GUIDE:
# 8B. Ruler Effectiveness and Competence

Status: draft; v4 objective-evidence questions require controlled rejudgment

## Chapter Identity

- Chapter: `8B`
- Category: `ruler_effectiveness_and_competence`
- Evidence strategy: one ruler-period dossier, researched across ten overlapping lenses
- Judge strategy: one chapter judge applies one comparative meter across all eligible rulers
- Rubric version: `chapter_8b_v4`

## Chapter Purpose

Assess how effectively the ruler defines a governing program, converts it into
state or ruling-network action, coordinates implementation, adapts, and advances
their own stated or reliably revealed objectives. The ten questions are evidence
lenses feeding one final 1-10 chapter score, not equal sub-scores.

This chapter is ideology-neutral. Moral worth and execution competence are
separate. A ruler may execute a cruel, aggressive, corrupt, or authoritarian
program competently and score highly in 8B while scoring very poorly in other
chapters. Never smuggle moral approval or condemnation into the competence score.

## Ten Evidence Lenses

| Lens | Simple question | Detailed research question | Priority evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| **8B.1 — Clear governing program** | Were the ruler's real goals clear enough to evaluate? | Did the ruler state or reliably reveal a sufficiently clear program in dated speeches, manifestos, strategies, directives, or formal acts to freeze and test its policy, ideological, power, and international goals? | **Rhetoric and representations**; **Formal acts and law** |
| **8B.2 — Turning goals into machinery** | Did the ruler turn goals into concrete plans, rules, resources, people, and institutions? | Did the ruler translate that program into enacted laws, budgets, appointments, timelines, institutions, regulations, and enforcement mechanisms within actual authority? | **Formal acts and law**; **Personnel**; **Implementation and operational conduct** |
| **8B.3 — Mobilizing the ruling system** | Did the ruler effectively mobilize the state and ruling network toward those goals? | Did executed resources and administrative records show effective mobilization of the state, party, military, coalition, or ruling network toward the ruler's chosen program? | **Resources**; **Personnel**; **Implementation and operational conduct** |
| **8B.4 — Capable implementers** | Did the ruler choose and manage people capable of carrying out the program? | Did the ruler appoint, empower, retain, and when necessary replace people capable of executing the program, whether professionals, technocrats, organizers, loyal operators, or coercive administrators? | **Personnel**; **Implementation and operational conduct**; **Outcomes** |
| **8B.5 — Coordination and control** | Did the ruler maintain coordination and follow-through across the system? | Did the ruler maintain documented coordination, territorial reach, milestone completion, and compliance across ministries, regions, institutions, security forces, and implementing agencies? | **Implementation and operational conduct**; **Outcomes** |
| **8B.6 — From plans to practice** | Did the ruler turn plans and announcements into real government action? | Did legislation, budgets, and directives become observable enforcement, services, projects, and institutional practice rather than remain slogans, plans, or symbolic acts? | **Formal acts and law**; **Implementation and operational conduct**; **Outcomes** |
| **8B.7 — Progress toward chosen goals** | Did results move toward the ruler's own goals? | Did outcome and distribution indicators move toward the ruler's frozen goals after accounting for baseline, realistic lag, authority, external shocks, and plausible causal alternatives? | **Outcomes**; **Implementation and operational conduct** |
| **8B.8 — Learning and correction** | Did the ruler adapt and correct course when methods failed? | Did audits, evaluations, and implementation failures lead the ruler to adapt methods, replace implementers, reallocate resources, and correct course? | **Personnel**; **Implementation and operational conduct**; **Outcomes** |
| **8B.9 — Managing crises and resistance** | Did the ruler handle crises and resistance without losing the chosen program? | Did formal decisions and implemented responses to crises, opposition, international relations, and institutional resistance preserve or advance the ruler's chosen objectives and durable control? | **Formal acts and law**; **Implementation and operational conduct**; **Outcomes** |
| **8B.10 — Durable goal achievement** | By the end, had the ruler achieved more of the program in a lasting way? | By the end of the period, had the ruler converted more of the frozen program into durable law, institutions, capacity, state practice, and achieved outcomes than at the start, accounting for failures and long-term fragility? | **Outcomes**; **Formal acts and law**; **Implementation and operational conduct** |

Priority evidence categories indicate promising evidence, not required coverage or an
exclusive source list. Missing categories do not invalidate a lens.

## Scope and Non-Goals

- Establish an explicit or reliably revealed program before judging execution.
  Repeated discriminating choices may reveal goals; hindsight speculation may not.
- Evaluate strategic clarity, operationalization, mobilization, personnel fit,
  coordination, implementation, outcomes, adaptation, crisis management, and
  durable progress as mutually informing evidence.
- Do not judge whether goals are democratic, peaceful, lawful, prosperous, humane,
  or personally honest. Those judgments belong in 1B-7B.
- Do not reward repression, propaganda, longevity, survival, economic growth,
  administrative size, or institutional quality by itself. Ask whether it
  effectively served the ruler's identified program.
- Do not assume failure from an outcome the ruler did not seek, except where
  maintaining basic government function was necessarily part of governing.
- Do not use the client matrix as evidence.

Before reading outcomes or assigning a score, freeze a portfolio of the ruler's major
stated or reliably revealed programs plus necessary core-government functions. Apply
the same breadth rule across regime types. Do not cherry-pick the easiest achieved
goal, reduce a democratic government to its broadest welfare promises, or reduce an
authoritarian government to survival, weapons, or power consolidation. Survival and
consolidation may be one material program component, never the entire portfolio by
default.

## Researcher Workflow and Evidence Themes

Follow the local-first researcher guide and source-confidence registry. Query the
exact case locally. WGI, V-Dem, BTI, budgets, and outcome indicators are useful
context or corroboration, but generic state capacity is not ruler effectiveness.

Research chronologically across themes rather than issuing one search per lens:

1. Identify stated programs from platforms, manifestos, coalition agreements,
   speeches, strategies, directives, budgets, and plans. Identify revealed goals
   only from repeated choices that distinguish them from rhetoric or speculation.
2. Trace conversion into priorities, resources, laws, institutions, appointments,
   timelines, enforcement, and measurable milestones.
3. Examine whether the state, party, military, coalition, bureaucracy, territorial
   network, or informal machinery actually mobilized, coordinated, and followed
   through; include bottlenecks and implementation gaps.
4. Assess whether personnel were capable for the assigned program. Ideological or
   coercive operators may be effective implementers in this narrow chapter even
   when their conduct is reprehensible elsewhere.
5. Compare promised/revealed objectives with implementation and outcomes after
   realistic lags, inherited trends, peer conditions, shocks, and resources.
6. Trace learning, replacement, reallocation, crisis response, resistance
   management, short-term gains, reversals, durability, and end-of-period position.

Prefer primary program and budget documents, legislation, administrative and
audit records, implementation data, credible outcome series, contemporaneous
records, serious academic histories, independent evaluations, and reputable
reporting. Official sources establish declared goals and formal actions but not
successful execution by themselves. Preserve failures, contradictions, abandoned
priorities, propaganda-only claims, alternative causal explanations, and adverse
side effects; record the latter for cross-chapter use without morally deducting
them in 8B.

Reuse each stable evidence item across relevant lenses. Natural-language coverage
and gaps are acceptable; publication must not depend on exact status terms,
sequential IDs, or exhaustive mapping syntax.

## Common Evidence-Item Semantics

Each material evidence item should carry a stable evidence ID; a concise factual
claim; source URL, title, publisher, and publication/access date where available;
source type and confidence with a short reason; a quotation or precise locator
when available; target-year/ruler-period fit; ruler attribution and
inherited-baseline relevance; its positive, negative, mixed, contextual, or
contrary role; and the supported lens IDs. Fields may be normalized later and
natural-language equivalents are acceptable. The substantive minimum is a
traceable source, intelligible claim, temporal fit, attribution, and evidence
role. One item may support multiple lenses without duplication.

## Attribution, Sparse Evidence, and Historical Cases

Separate ruler capability from inherited state capacity, predecessor programs,
subordinate initiative, favorable trends, external patrons, and luck. Assess
formal/informal control, tenure, coalition and institutional constraints,
resources, shocks, lag, ownership of appointments and priorities, response to
implementation information, and plausible counterfactual trajectory.

For obscure or historical rulers, later scholarship, archives, diplomatic
records, budgets, institutional histories, and observable implementation may be
more informative than missing modern indicators. Oral or informal programs can
be evidenced by repeated choices. Sparse evidence should reduce confidence and
widen the defensible range, not force a neutral score or invalidate the case.
Short tenure may permit judgment of direction and mobilization while leaving
outcomes unresolved. Closed systems may document plans abundantly while hiding
failures; official volume is not proof of execution.

## Holistic 1-10 Chapter Rubric

Synthesize the causal chain from program through action to durable progress.
Lenses are redundant and qualitatively weighted: clear plans with no execution
cannot score highly; favorable outcomes without ruler-attributable direction or
action warrant limited credit; a major strategic collapse can outweigh several
administrative successes. Never calculate a simple mean or score by citation
count.

Strategic clarity, centralized control, personnel discipline, and planning without
independently evidenced implementation and goal progress cannot exceed 5. Scores above
5 require attributable implementation plus progress across material parts of the
frozen portfolio. Scores above 7 additionally require independent evidence of outcomes,
durability, and adaptation rather than official architecture, coercion, longevity, or
survival alone. Harmful programs remain morally neutral here, but they must still show
actual advancement rather than mere capacity to command.

| Score | Chapter anchor |
|---:|---|
| 1 | No coherent program or effective governing control, or catastrophic inability to mobilize and execute: recurring chaos, paralysis, collapse, or decisive movement away from the ruler's own objectives. |
| 2-3 | Weak or unstable direction and serious operational failure; plans remain largely symbolic, coordination repeatedly breaks down, adaptation is poor, and major objectives substantially recede. |
| 4-5 | Mixed competence: some clear priorities and real delivery coexist with major implementation gaps, inconsistent control, failed initiatives, weak adaptation, or fragile/temporary results. |
| 6-7 | Generally effective direction and execution across important priorities, with meaningful mobilization and progress but notable delays, failures, constraints, reversals, or durability concerns. |
| 8-9 | Strong strategic-operational alignment, capable personnel and coordination, adaptive implementation, and substantial durable advancement of the ruler's program across major priorities. |
| 10 | Exceptional, sustained mastery of direction, mobilization, execution, adaptation, crisis/resistance management, and durable goal realization under demanding conditions. |

These anchors apply equally to benevolent and harmful programs. The rationale
must separately name the program and state that moral effects are evaluated in
other chapters.

## Confidence

Report confidence separately on a 0-100 scale. Consider program identification,
ruler attribution, causal trace from inputs to implementation/outcomes, source
independence, time fit and lag, external-shock controls, source/theme diversity,
closed-system opacity, and contrary explanations. Missing lenses lower confidence
rather than automatically lowering the chapter score. Give a defensible score
range where evidence or attribution is materially uncertain.

## One-Judge Comparative Workflow

One judge owns 8B across all eligible rulers for the target year/period:

1. Read every 8B dossier, identify each ruler's explicit or reliably revealed
   program, and quarantine identity failures—not sparse evidence.
2. Separate moral evaluation from execution before scoring. Record cross-chapter
   harms without deducting them here.
3. Establish batch anchors spanning democratic and authoritarian, beneficial and
   harmful, modern and historical, high- and low-capacity contexts.
4. Assign tentative holistic scores along the program-to-results chain, then
   compare neighbors for consistent treatment of inherited capacity, resources,
   shocks, tenure, lag, luck, resistance, and durability.
5. Revisit cases dominated by generic governance indicators, official claims,
   survival/tenure, favorable outcomes without attribution, or moral halo/horns.
6. Emit one chapter score, confidence/range, identified program, rationale,
   strongest execution and failure evidence, unresolved causal alternatives,
   missing themes, and review flag.

## Common Chapter-Judge Output Envelope

The judge returns one object per ruler using these exact semantic field names so
all eight chapter outputs can be normalized by the same inexpensive formatter:

- `chapter_id`: `8B`
- `rubric_version`: `chapter_8b_v4`
- `calibration_batch_id` and `calibrated_against`
- `score_1_to_10`, or null with `insufficient_evidence_reason`
- `confidence_score` and `plausible_score_range`
- `decisive_positive_evidence` and `decisive_negative_evidence`, referencing
  stable evidence IDs
- `inherited_baseline_and_constraints` and `ruler_attribution`
- `supported_lenses` and `missing_or_weak_lenses`
- `contrary_evidence`
- `source_mix` and `structured_prior_summary`
- `chapter_rationale`, `lower_anchor_rejected`, and `higher_anchor_rejected`
- `manual_review_required`, `manual_review_reason_type`, and `manual_review_reason`

Harmless serialization differences can be repaired after judgment, but the
semantic envelope must be complete. A null score is reserved for evidence too
thin or untraceable to support any responsible range, unresolved identity, or
attribution too uncertain to judge—not merely missing lenses or flexible coverage
wording. The rationale must identify the program and state explicitly that its
moral worth did not affect the execution score.

## Chapter-Specific Calibration

Use common calibration and bias checks where meaningful, plus:

- `rubric_version`: `chapter_8b_v4`
- `program_basis`: `explicit`, `mostly_explicit`, `mixed`, `mostly_revealed`,
  `speculative`, or `unclear`
- `program_type`: list from `policy`, `state_building`, `regime_control`,
  `power_consolidation`, `international_influence`, `survival`, or `other`
- `strategic_clarity`: `none`, `weak`, `partial`, `clear`, `exceptional`, or `unclear`
- `operationalization`: `absent`, `weak`, `mixed`, `strong`, `exceptional`, or `unclear`
- `mobilization_and_coordination`: `failed`, `weak`, `mixed`, `strong`,
  `exceptional`, or `unclear`
- `adaptation`: `counterproductive`, `rigid`, `mixed`, `effective`,
  `exceptional`, `not_observed`, or `unclear`
- `goal_progress`: `major_reversal`, `net_regression`, `mixed`, `substantial`,
  `exceptional`, or `too_early_or_unclear`
- `durability`: `collapsed`, `fragile`, `mixed`, `durable`, `highly_durable`,
  `too_early`, or `unclear`
- `ruler_attribution`: concise assessment of ownership, authority, inherited
  capacity, subordinate action, shocks, luck, and alternative causes
- `morality_separation_check`: explicit statement that approval/disapproval of
  objectives did not alter the score
- `lens_strengths`, `lens_gaps`, and `defensible_score_range`

Explicitly check state-capacity inheritance, survivor bias, outcome/luck bias,
propaganda and closed-system opacity, documentation advantages, hindsight-defined
goals, short-tenure/lag bias, moral halo/horns, and whether coercion was mistaken
for competence without evidence of goal advancement.

## Smoke-Test Set

These are calibration roles, not predetermined scores.

| Ruler | Country | Period | Calibration purpose |
|---|---|---|---|
| Lee Kuan Yew | Singapore | 1965-1990 | long-horizon direction, state capacity, durability |
| Deng Xiaoping | China | 1978-1992 | revealed/pragmatic program and adaptation |
| Adolf Hitler | Germany | 1933-1945 | harmful program, early execution, strategic catastrophe |
| Joseph Stalin | Soviet Union | 1928-1953 | coercive implementation and moral-separation stress test |
| Margaret Thatcher | United Kingdom | 1979-1990 | explicit democratic program, resistance, and durable change |
| Mikhail Gorbachev | Soviet Union | 1985-1991 | adaptation, partial achievement, loss of control, disputed goals |
| Boris Yeltsin | Russia | 1991-1999 | crisis, weak coordination, inherited collapse, mixed reforms |
| Paul Kagame | Rwanda | 2000-2020 | development and regime-control goals with attribution concerns |
| Volodymyr Zelenskyy | Ukraine | 2019-2023 | program transformed by extreme external shock |
| Liz Truss | United Kingdom | 2022 | short tenure, explicit program, rapid implementation failure |
| Franklin D. Roosevelt | United States | 1933-1945 | institutional mobilization across depression and war |
| Mobutu Sese Seko | Zaire | 1965-1997 | survival/personal-rule effectiveness versus state deterioration |
| Ellen Johnson Sirleaf | Liberia | 2006-2018 | post-conflict rebuilding, donor attribution, and woman ruler |
| Sirimavo Bandaranaike | Sri Lanka | 1960-1965 | historical woman ruler, coalition/state capacity, and non-Western evidence |
| Sylvanus Olympio | Togo | 1960-1963 | short, lower-visibility postcolonial program and sparse archives |

## Acceptance Checklist

- Missing implementation evidence never establishes ineffectiveness; it produces a
  null when no ruler-attributed execution fact remains. Scores above `5` require
  observable implementation or coordination, and scores above `7` additionally
  require independent evidence of attributable outcomes, adaptation, or durable
  progress across more than one material program component.

- All ten authoritative lenses are present verbatim and serve one holistic score.
- Ideology-neutrality is explicit and moral worth is separated from execution in
  research, scoring, calibration, and rationale.
- An explicit or reliably revealed program is established before execution is
  judged; hindsight speculation is labeled and constrained.
- Local-first order, citations, source profiling, and run profile are retained.
- Evidence items satisfy the common traceability, temporal-fit, attribution,
  evidence-role, and lens-link semantics.
- Ruler action is separated from inherited capacity, subordinates, luck, trends,
  shocks, tenure, and realistic outcome lags.
- Missing lenses reduce confidence rather than invalidate or mechanically depress
  the score.
- One judge compares all rulers using one meter; lenses are neither equally
  weighted nor mechanically averaged.
- Final output uses the common chapter-judge envelope, including adjacent-anchor
  comparisons and an explicit manual-review flag.
- Smoke testing and revision occur before production activation.

De acuerdo con los datos del BEM 2021, la autoridad migratoria\n   canalizó a las entidades seleccionadas 53,506 NNAM. Por su parte\n   dichas entidades reportan una atención a 32,115 NNAM en ese mismo\n   año, es decir, el 60.0% de la población canalizada.\n21. Tanto por efecto de la pandemia como de las reformas a las Leyes de\n   Migración y de Refugio, Protección Complementaria y Asilo Político, la\n   población de NNAM atendida en los CAS de los sistemas DIF creció de\n   manera desproporcionada entre 2020 y 2021. También hubo cambios\n   en su composición. Los menores de 12 años pasaron de un porcentaje\n   de 10.1% en 2021, a 46.0% en 2022. De igual forma, las mujeres\n   representaron 23.2% y 41.6% en un año y otro; y los acompañados\n   subieron de 5.7% a 16.1% en el último año. Ello denota el efecto de las\n   caravanas y el acompañamiento en núcleos familiares en la\n   composición de los flujos migratorios.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                             105\n\n22. En 2021, los sistemas estatales reportaron 23,427 NNAM en la categoría\n   sin dato, en los rubros de preferencia sexual y expresión de género, es\n   decir, el 73.9%. Ello puede significar que no se hace un adecuado\n   levantamiento de la información de cada beneficiario al momento de\n   su ingreso o una falta de preparación del personal en materia de\n   equidad de género.\n23. Se informa de sólo un caso de origen indígena y ninguno de afro\n   descendencia. La ausencia de datos es incongruente con la\n   procedencia mayoritaria de una región con alto porcentaje de\n   población indígena como lo son los países del Triángulo Norte de\n   Centroamérica y la significativa participación de países caribeños y\n   sudamericanos en la composición de los flujos migratorios a través de\n   nuestro país.\n24. Las cifras sobre los solicitantes de la condición de refugiado son muy\n   bajas: 2.4% en 2020 y 1.4% en 2021. Las cifras de la COMAR en cuanto\n   a los solicitantes de la condición de refugiado representan el 49.8% de\n   las personas presentadas o canalizadas en 2020, y el 42.4% en 2021.\n   Llama la atención también que el número de NNAM regularizados\n   supere el de solicitantes. En 2020, 11 solicitantes y 16 regularizados; en\n   2021, fueron 430 y 511.\n25. En 2020, prácticamente el total de la población canalizada (95.2%)\n   procedían de los países del Triángulo Norte de Centroamérica. En 2021,\n   esta participación se redujo a 52.6% debido a la significativa presencia\n   de la diáspora haitiana, así como de países como Cuba, Nicaragua y\n   Venezuela en los flujos migratorios. Ello explica la necesidad de\n   traductores en los CAS para atender de manera más adecuada a los\n   usuarios.\n26. En el mismo año, atendiendo la demanda de NNAM, los DIF estatales\n   han presentado limitaciones de todo tipo: insumos, infraestructura, de\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                106\n\npersonal y de presupuesto. Ello queda confirmado por los estados de\n   Veracruz y Tabasco, que canalizan el exceso de demanda a los\n   albergues municipales. Cabría evaluar si las instalaciones alternativas\n   cuentan con los medios necesarios para brindar a la población una\n   atención adecuada.\n27. De la misma forma, la aplicación del principio del interés de la niñez se\n   realiza de forma diversa en las distintas entidades. Si bien es cierto, los\n   estados han manifestado contar con equipos multidisciplinarios para la\n   elaboración de diagnósticos y el diseño de planes de restitución de\n   derechos, no existe la garantía de que efectivamente cumplan con los\n   objetivos para los que fueron creados.\n28. En relación con la información recibida respecto del Reporte de\n   Resultados del Mecanismo de Participación sobre la Ruta de Protección\n   Integral de los Derechos de Niñas, Niños y Adolescentes en Situación\n   de Migración coordinado por la Secretaría Ejecutiva del SIPINNA, la\n   CNDH observa:\n       a. Se reconoce el importante ejercicio de dar voz a niñas, niños y\n           adolescentes migrantes, de conformidad con la Ley General de\n           los Derechos de Niñas, Niños y Adolescentes y en atención a su\n           interés superior.\n       b. Aunque se entienden las dificultades metodológicas, la CNDH\n           reitera que al no incluir a niñas, niños y adolescentes no\n           acompañados y al no incluir en la muestra a los Centros de\n           Asistencia Social del SN DIF y los SE DIF, puede haber algún\n           sesgo en los resultados de la aplicación,\n       c. En cuanto a los resultados, éstos arrojan que en las etapas 0 y 1\n           de la ruta hay una desatención de las necesidades de seguridad,\n           protección y que frente a esto se hace indispensable contar con\n           atención de primeros auxilios psicológicos.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                 107\n\nd. Resultan preocupantes los resultados sobre la etapa 2 de la ruta,\n     referente al interés superior de niñas, niños y adolescentes, ya\n     que demuestra que en general, su opinión no es solicitada para\n     determinarlo. Esto es, desde la percepción de las autoridades\n     encargadas de su atención y cuidado, NNAM no tienen la\n     capacidad de opinar; es indispensable crear capacitaciones que\n     incidan en la forma en la que los funcionarios que tienen\n     contacto con los NNA los conciban como seres humanos\n     integrales.\ne. Evidentemente, si las autoridades no pidieron la opinión de\n     NNA sobre su interés superior, se reduce significativamente la\n     posibilidad de que se garanticen las medidas relativas a la etapa\n     3, de restitución de derechos.\nf.   Niñas, niños y adolescentes en movilidad consideran que estar\n     en un albergue, acompañados de su familia, significa una\n     restitución de derechos. Tanto para esta etapa, como para la 4,\n     referente a la preparación y transición para la vida\n     independiente, las actividades que se realizan fuera del albergue\n     sí dificultan la integración y restitución de derechos.\ng. La aplicación de los instrumentos y el análisis de los resultados\n     permite llegar a recomendaciones básicas, haciendo énfasis en\n     la importancia de capacitación y el cambio de paradigma que\n     mira a NNAM como sujetos “menores” sobre los que\n     funcionarios y profesionistas pueden decidir sin necesidad de\n     escucharlos, para acceder a otro en el que se les escuche y se\n     tomen las decisiones junto con ellas y ellos.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                         108", "excerpt_sha256": "ed630fb589a85ef9cca51fa47d7489dd225adc968ff7bb77493069bef98bde71", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0015-E013", "source_id": "SRC-796f2a8cf19b045b", "url": "https://mexico.un.org/sites/default/files/2024-04/Informe%202023%20ONU%20MEXICO-abril12_3.pdf", "title": "Informe 2023 ONU México", "publisher": "Equipo de País de las Naciones Unidas en México", "raw_sha256": "4637892399c6bdb5f7fbb26f4f1d3b77202205dd45dbe14fef7b49365c3770ce", "fact_summary": "The UN reported 2023 labor and inclusion initiatives: more than 35,000 refugees and asylum seekers accessed formal employment through the Local Integration Program, 90% of participants reported formal employment and children's school integration, more than 8,700 women gained skills through a multi-year program, and rural women's networks and value-chain projects expanded economic opportunities.", "question_ids": ["5B.4", "5B.8", "6B.10", "6B.2", "6B.3", "8B.6", "8B.7"], "polarity": "favorable", "period_fit": "The evidence includes outcomes reported in 2023, though some initiatives began before 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The programs involved Mexican federal, state, and local authorities with UN, civil-society, and private partners; It warned that achieving the planned 2025 consolidation would require additional measures, and called for higher non-oil revenue, better-targeted social protection, more efficient education spending, and stronger fiscal transparency.", "question_ids": ["5B.1", "5B.10", "5B.3", "5B.6", "5B.7", "5B.8", "6B.2", "6B.5", "8B.2"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The budget was proposed in 2023 by the outgoing AMLO administration, making the warning directly relevant to the target year.", "ruler_attribution": "The IMF attributes the budget proposal to the Mexican authorities under AMLO.", "limitations": ["The main fiscal effects were projected for 2024 and therefore are not fully observed in 2023."], "start_unit": 14, "end_unit": 15, "locator": "page 14 through page 15", "exact_excerpt": "MEXICO\n\n\n\n20.      The 2024 budget is procyclical, proposing a significant expansion in the fiscal position.\nWith a fiscal impulse of 2.4 percent of GDP, the fiscal stance for 2024 is unhelpfully procyclical at a\ntime when the economy is above potential and inflation is not yet back to target. The budget\ncombines a realistic revenue forecast with sizable increases in current spending items—notably for\nwages, pensions, and social spending—resulting in a deficit of 5.4 percent of GDP. The medium-\nterm budget framework envisages a large consolidation in 2025. While the authorities have a track\nrecord of meeting targets, achieving the 2025 fiscal target would necessitate additional measures of\naround 1.7 percent of GDP. A more prudent fiscal stance in 2024 would have been desirable—to\navoid creating the conditions for higher interest rates, a stronger currency, and a higher debt-to-\nGDP ratio. The current fiscal stance puts additional burden on monetary policy in achieving the\ntargeted disinflation. Under current policies, public debt would rise to 54.7 percent of GDP at end-\n2024 and stabilize at around 56 percent over the medium-term, conditional on the needed\nadjustment over the medium term. Public debt is assessed to be sustainable with high probability,\nwith fiscal risks mitigated by the country access to international and domestic debt markets.\n\n21.     The explicit inclusion of support for Pemex in the budget increases transparency and\nshould help facilitate a discussion of supporting the company versus other budgetary\npriorities. However, continued budgetary support to Pemex should be conditioned on a credible\nplan to ensure Pemex’s commercial viability. Furthermore, Mexico’s public sector contains many\nother entities that are distinct from the Federal government and the complex financial transactions\nbetween public sector entities—including through trust funds, state-affiliated enterprises, and\ndevelopment corporations—argues for a better reporting of consolidated fiscal outturns that is\naligned with international accounting practices. Such an improvement in statistics would allow for\nbetter appreciation of the trade-offs among competing demands on public resources as well as help\nidentify the size of potential fiscal risks and contingent liabilities.\n\n22. A stronger medium-term fiscal framework would increase the credibility of fiscal\npolicy and create greater fiscal space to respond to downside risks. A framework that more\nclearly links the desired public debt level to annual budgetary decisions would greatly increase the\ntransparency and accountability of fiscal policymaking. A broader revamp of the fiscal framework\nalong these lines could include: (i) a well-calibrated debt anchor, (ii) introducing a credible medium-\nterm budget framework; (iii) allowing for more countercyclical fiscal policy, potentially through the\nbuild-up of additional fiscal buffers; (iv) clarifying escape clauses from the fiscal rules and limiting\nthem to specified exceptional circumstances; (v) increasing the analysis of the impact of policies; and\n(vi) improving fiscal forecasting capacity.\n\n24.      Targeted fiscal policies would help address gender gaps. The authorities have increased\nefforts to boost gender equality but would benefit from: (i) gender budgeting, with an integration of\ngender policies into the budget through a Gender Impact Assessment; (ii) better collection of\ngender-related data on social and economic outturns; (iii) increasing support for maternal health\nand childcare; (iv) improving allocations to the most effective social protection programs; (v) more\ntargeted education support programs; (vi) abolishing the minimum contribution requirement for\nfunded pensions; and (vii) adopting gender-sensitive employment practices in the public sector.\n\n25.      Authorities’ views. The 2024 budget submitted to Congress is looser than previously\nplanned. However, this stems from an increased expenditure related to the current administration’s\ncommitments—principally, pensions, wages, and healthcare reforms. Additionally, capital\nexpenditure to complete key infrastructure projects will help Mexico seize the opportunity of\nnearshoring and boost connectivity and growth in the developing southern region. Needed\nconsolidation in 2025, beyond the expiration of one-off capital expenses, could be met with\ncontinued improvements in tax efficiency and normalization of monetary policy. Pemex has been\nincluded in the budget to improve transparency, support its cash position, and guarantee its debt\npayments. The resources transferred to Pemex change the cash position but neither affect the\noverall public sector balance nor hinder the Federal Government expenditure, as the transfers\nrequire Pemex’s balance improvement by the same amount. The current administration intends not\nto raise tax rates. Measures are being undertaken to address gender gaps, including plans to\nimprove government provision of childcare.\n\nB. Durably Bringing Inflation Back to Target\n\n26.      A proactive approach to monetary policy has helped contain inflationary pressures.\nBanxico was one of the first major central banks to begin a tightening cycle, raising its policy rate\nfrom 4 percent in June 2021 to 11.25 percent in March 2023. The real rate is now firmly in\ncontractionary territory. Inflation expectations have remained well-anchored, underscoring the\ncredibility of the inflation targeting regime.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND          11", "excerpt_sha256": "b1b320b421002ef5926ca2d38448b864ab27dd87f949cdf52f151506c4394bb1", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0016-E016", "source_id": "SRC-684988a395d1a128", "url": "https://www.banxico.org.mx/publications-and-press/imf-article-iv-consultation-for-mexico/%7B2B9230B7-CC63-5B20-E173-F5E94D116BC9%7D.pdf", "title": "IMF Country Report No. 23/356 — Mexico", "publisher": "Banco de México / International Monetary Fund", "raw_sha256": "f93592f437a264c95c4c753720221642f0dd636d55e012cef97b95d1b59a294a", "fact_summary": "The IMF found Mexico well positioned for nearshoring but noted that announced investment had not yet produced clear aggregate macroeconomic effects. It reported that authorities had a broad supply-side agenda involving infrastructure, trade, minimum wages, labor law, female participation, crime and corruption, finance, formalization, and energy, while warning that regulatory and supply-side constraints remained.", "question_ids": ["5B.1", "5B.10", "5B.4", "5B.6", "8B.1", "8B.2", "8B.7"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The assessment is based on developments through 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The agenda is attributed to Mexican authorities during AMLO's administration.", "limitations": ["The source does not establish which agenda items were enacted or implemented by López Obrador personally."], "start_unit": 20, "end_unit": 22, "locator": "page 20 through page 22", "exact_excerpt": "MEXICO\n\n\n\n     experience with LTV and DSTI. A first draft on progress on these two workstreams is expected by\n     end-2023 and by March-2024, respectively.\n\n35.     The authorities should continue to address the outstanding legal gaps in the Anti-\nMoney Laundering/Combating the Financing of Terrorism (AML/CFT) framework and ensure\nits effective implementation. Substantive progress has been made in addressing a majority of the\nlegal deficiencies identified in the 2018 Mutual Evaluation Report of Mexico. 2 However, deficiencies\nremain in relation to the AML/CFT regulation and supervision of Designated Non-Financial\nBusinesses and Professions (DNFBPs), reporting of suspicious transactions, establishing an effective\ncash-couriers regime, and the inability of the CNBV to suspend or withdraw a banking license for\nAML/CFT breaches. Mexico should further create a beneficial ownership register to ensure corporate\ntransparency and mitigate the risks of misuse of legal entities, including in relation to public\nprocurement. The CNBVs risk-based supervision should ensure effective oversight over cross-border\nflows including verifying information on international wire transfers and the activities of money\nremitters. The SAT does not currently have the necessary legal powers to sufficiently supervise and\nenforce compliance by the DNFBPs in line with the risk-based approach. Adequate resources and\nbudget should be allocated to the prosecution to ensure enforcement of money laundering and\nunderlying predicate offenses. Mexico should consider to further leverage the available AML\nmeasures to better tackle financial crimes, such as corruption (see below) and tax evasion. The\nAML/CFT agencies, including the CNBV, SAT, the Financial Intelligence Unit (FIU), law enforcement,\nas well as anti-corruption bodies, should enhance their collaboration.\n\n36.      Authorities’ views. The banking system is healthy and able to withstand various stress\nscenarios, with increased capital levels and near-record lows NPLs at commercial banks. Given their\nstrong balance sheets, banks can expand credit further without increasing risks to the system. While\nsome NBFIs have experienced difficulties over the past year, the sector is small and does not pose a\nsystemic risk. Progress has been made in implementing several recommendations from the 2022\nFSAP, but some recommendations—such as strengthening the autonomy of regulatory agencies\nand allowing for consolidated supervision of financial conglomerates—would require legislation.\nRecent initiatives include enhancements in monitoring and responding to cyber-attacks, including\nvia simulation exercises, and formalizing coordination and collaboration protocols. Banxico’s\nliquidity stress test framework has been updated. Earlier this year, the authority responsible for\ndeposit insurance (IPAB) reformed its rules to shorten the resolution planning period for DSIBs.\nContinuing to carry out AML/CFT reforms is a priority, with relevant improvements made in the laws\nand their effective implementation in practice.\n\nD. Making Growth Inclusive and Sustainable\n\n37.    Deeper reforms are needed to capitalize on the potential gains from new trade\nopportunities. Despite a significant increase in openness over the past 30 years, per capita output\ngrowth has lagged that of Mexico’s G20 peers. Recent supply chain diversification has led Mexico to\n\n2 See https://www.fatf-gafi.org/en/countries/detail/Mexico.html. For the current status on legal changes, see the 2023\n\nFollow-Up Report of Mexico.\n\n\n\n16   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\ntake over from China as the U.S. largest import partner.\nHowever, supply-side constraints are already becoming\nevident. Removing these constraints will be essential to\nattract new supply chains and capitalize on the domestic\ncontent rules of recent U.S. fiscal legislation (Box 3).\n\n38.      The authorities have a broad supply-side reform\nagenda. This comprises the completion of large\ninfrastructure projects, trade promotion, further increases in\nthe minimum wage to reduce inequality, and implementing\nthe 2019 labor law (that includes a new labor resolution mechanism meant to reduce delays and\nlegal uncertainties). Additional efforts could comprise policies that facilitate greater female labor\nparticipation (Box 4), tackle corruption and crime, improve access to finance, further reduce the\ncosts of formalization, and strengthen the supply of energy.\n                                               Worldwide Governance Indicators, 2022\n                                                             (Index)\n     0.5\n\n     0.0\n\n    -0.5\n\n    -1.0\n                                                                    Mexico       Latam\n    -1.5\n                Voice and        Political Stability     Government        Regulatory Quality       Rule of Law      Control of\n              Accountability      and Absence of         Effectiveness                                               Corruption\n                                Violence/Terrorism\n\n    Sources: Daniel Kaufmann (Natural Resource Governance Institute and Brookings Institution) and Aart Kraay (World Bank), and IMF\n    staff calculations.\n    Note: Whiskers report 95 percent confidence interval. These are not available for the Latam average.\n\n\n\n                                               Box 3. Evidence on Nearshoring1\n Mexico is expected to benefit from the growing interest in “nearshoring.” Nearshoring—which refers to\n relocation of production and supply chains closer to final consumption to reduce risks of supply chain\n disruptions—has gained prominence in the policy arena given geopolitical fragmentation, disruptions during\n the pandemic, and Russia’s invasion of Ukraine. Mexico seems well-placed, due to its proximity to the U.S.,\n macroeconomic stability, relatively low labor costs, an existing supply chain ecosystem, and membership in\n USMCA, particularly given the tightening of regional rules of origin in certain sectors.\n While many nearshoring-related investment plans have been announced, their impact is not yet evident in\n macroeconomic indicators. Based on news and market reports over the last two years, staff has identified\n more than US$11 billion of announced FDI plans, generally related to the automobile sector, electric vehicles\n (EV) and batteries, circuits, and automation. Some appear to be on the intensive margin (expanding\n capacity), some on the extensive (e.g., new EV plants), and some have elements of both. Investment\n horizons, where information is available, are around 2-3 years. However, delays from announcement to\n implementation are to be expected; it is difficult so far to pinpoint an impact of nearshoring in aggregate\n data. Trade signals are mixed; real exports declined in the first half of 2023 while Mexico has gained market\n shares in the U.S. Gross capital formation is just recovering, with investment in machinery and equipment\n surpassing pre-pandemic levels only recently. Inward FDI flows are lower in the first half of 2023 compared\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                                INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND              17\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n                                                   Box 3. A Deep Dive on Gender-Related Policy Issues\n  As part of the 2023 Article IV consultation, an interdepartmental team (comprising FAD, LEG, SPR, and WHD)\n  did a “deep dive” on gender-related issues, focusing on constraints on Mexico’s growth potential. This\n  exercise consisted of three separate projects (see Selected Issues):\n i.       Key legal and institutional issues to support women’s economic empowerment. The chapter examines\n          measures to further reduce gender gaps, highlighting possible legal reforms to close gender gaps by\n          expanding women’s rights—with equal pay for equal work; providing maternity, paternity, and parental\n          leave; and by addressing challenges of unpaid care work and women’s pension.\nii.       Fiscal policies to tackle gender issues. This chapter outlines a range of fiscal measures that could be\n          undertaken—in budgeting, revenue administration, and expenditure— to help close these gaps. It\n          shows that targeted policies can effectively close gender gaps and enhance social and economic\n          wellbeing.\niii.      Policies to boost female labor force participation. This chapter uses an OLG model calibrated to Mexico\n          to describe constraints on female labor force participation.\n          •    The analysis suggests that implementation of childcare policies alone could increase female labor\n               force participation by about 10 percentage points. This goes to 11 and 13 percentage points when\n               childcare is combined with higher education and anti-discriminatory practices, respectively.\n  Given the disparity in educational attainment, the impact of implementing such policies is more pronounced\n  in rural areas compared to urban regions.\n\n\n\n18        INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND", "excerpt_sha256": "539cdddb92c8f319a9ecd4da4405eeecee15f2ba7499b8931842a5a759fc975a", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0016-E017", "source_id": "SRC-684988a395d1a128", "url": "https://www.banxico.org.mx/publications-and-press/imf-article-iv-consultation-for-mexico/%7B2B9230B7-CC63-5B20-E173-F5E94D116BC9%7D.pdf", "title": "IMF Country Report No. 23/356 — Mexico", "publisher": "Banco de México / International Monetary Fund", "raw_sha256": "f93592f437a264c95c4c753720221642f0dd636d55e012cef97b95d1b59a294a", "fact_summary": "Mexico's minimum wage rose 80% in real terms since 2018; the IMF said early increases likely raised low-income wages but warned later increases could increase informality or reduce job creation. Moderation in the growth of the minimum\nwage would help mitigate potential adverse effects on\nformal employment. The minimum wage has increased\nby 80 percent in real terms since 2018 and now stands at\naround one-half of the average formal-sector wage. The\ninitial increases in the minimum wage likely raised wages\nfor lower income workers but, more recently, the share of\nlow-wage workers in the informal sector is increasing\n(suggesting higher minimum wages are likely negatively\naffecting employment outcomes). Further large minimum\nwage would likely increase informality and/or reduce job creation for lower income workers. Beyond\ncontaining minimum wage increases, informality could be reduced by lowering restrictions to\nlayoffs, removing adverse incentives in the pension system, and reducing the regulatory costs of\nformalizing a business.\n\n44.    Strengthening logistic and trade services would\nsupport private investment in tradable sectors.\nImprovements could focus on streamlining custom\nprocedures, easing licenses and permits procedures in road\ntransport and custom brokerage, and removing foreign direct\ninvestment restrictions in the transport sector. Streamlining\nbusiness regulations at the same time as governance\nimprovements are being made could magnify and accelerate\npotential growth gains. 3\n\n45.     Authorities’ views. Mexico’s open trade policy,\nstrategic investments in infrastructure, labor laws, and\nminimum-wage policy have promoted equitable growth, formal jobs, and labor force participation,\nparticularly for women. These features should extend through the country as nearshoring\nmaterializes. Despite progress, gender gaps do exist, and there is a need to further strengthen\npolicies to boost female labor force participation, in particular, reforming the childcare system and\naddressing the implementation gaps of the existing legal framework. Low financial depth indicators\nmay mask firms separately obtaining financing from suppliers. A new law has been passed to\nprovide more flexibility for customers to switch banks and encourage competition.\n\nE. Mitigating Climate Change and Increasing Energy Supply\n\n46.      In November 2022, Mexico increased its 2030 greenhouse gas (GHG) emission\nreduction target from 22 percent to 30 percent. This is a material contribution to the global\neffort, as Mexico’s GHG emissions amounted to 1.4 percent of global emissions in 2020. Achieving\nMexico’s Nationally Determined Contribution will require ambitious measures. The authorities’\n\n\n3 Budina et al., 2023, IMF Staff Discussion Note, No. 2023/007.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                  INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND      21\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\nstrategy comprises energy, transportation, waste management, residential sector, land use, land use\nchange, forestry, agriculture, and industry. Priorities include combating deforestation and an\nambitious zero-emission vehicle sales target (to 50 percent by 2030). The authorities have launched\na new plan to promote green finance, and initiated efforts to issue green bonds to support green\npublic spending projects. They have introduced a carbon tax and are working on implementing an\nemissions-trading system. However, the carbon tax rate remains modest at US$3.3 per ton, carbon\ntaxes implemented by states are still at low effective rates, and the excise fuel tax is set at a relatively\nlow rate.\n\n              Mexico Projected GHG Emissions 1/                         Change in Emissions Needed to Achieve NDC 2/\n                 (Percent of global emissions)                                    (Percent relative to baseline)\n      2.0                                                               World average\n              1.7       1.7       1.7                       Projected\n                                            1.6                  1.6           Mexico\n                                                      1.4                    Australia\n      1.5\n                                                                             Germany\n                                                                              Canada\n      1.0                                                                       Japan\n                                                                                 Brazil\n                                                                            Indonesia\n      0.5                                                                       Russia\n                                                                                 India\n                                                                                China\n      0.0\n                                                                         United States\n             2000      2005      2010      2015      2020       2030\n                                                                                          -50   -40   -30   -20    -10       0\n     Sources: UNFCCC;
