There is a need to create incentives to expand renewable energy supply. The 2013\nenergy reform had attracted private firms to Mexico to develop renewable energy capacity.\nHowever, regulatory hurdles have disrupted operations, disincentivized investments, and slowed the\nenergy transition. Reopening the energy sector to private competitors while increasing carbon\npricing would catalyze the development of renewable energy and incentivize the transfer of foreign\ntechnology in the sector. 4 The authorities’ ongoing plans to promote green finance and issue green\nbonds can further catalyze these initiatives. As documented in the FSAP, climate-related risk analysis\nfor the financial sector supports an early transition to a low-carbon economy.\n\n48.     Subsidizing investments by Pemex risks creating costly stranded assets. Public transfers\nto Pemex continue to support investments in oil exploration and refining. At WTI prices of US$80\nper barrel, about one sixth of Mexico’s current oil fields would be non-economic. Given that\noperation costs in Mexico are higher than among most of its competitors, Mexico is at considerable\nrisk from a structural decline in world oil prices and can face higher transition costs, especially in a\ndelayed climate mitigation scenario. 5 For example, if climate-mitigation initiatives were to reduce oil\nprices to US$60, more than one quarter of production in Mexico would be uneconomical, leading to\n\n4 The National Renewable Energy Laboratory finds significant unexploited potential for solar and wind generation in\n\nMexico with projects being feasible across the country, lessening the need for distribution investments.\n5 This risk could be sharper to the scenario analyzed in Annex III, as risks from lower production of oil would be\n\ncompounded by a lower price.\n\n\n22     INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND", "excerpt_sha256": "43410eaebcb032f210561deb33e383d7b6fb6e79fdfb6c81e8683480d344f602", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0016-E021", "source_id": "SRC-684988a395d1a128", "url": "https://www.banxico.org.mx/publications-and-press/imf-article-iv-consultation-for-mexico/%7B2B9230B7-CC63-5B20-E173-F5E94D116BC9%7D.pdf", "title": "IMF Country Report No. 23/356 — Mexico", "publisher": "Banco de México / International Monetary Fund", "raw_sha256": "f93592f437a264c95c4c753720221642f0dd636d55e012cef97b95d1b59a294a", "fact_summary": "The IMF found corruption among Mexico's highest money-laundering risks and reported that the anti-corruption system had legal and institutional foundations, but high-level corruption prosecutions were absent, enforcement was inadequate, beneficial-ownership information unreliable, and non-financial gatekeepers lacked required PEP due-diligence obligations. The IMF recommended stronger coordination, resources, sanctions, asset recovery, and verification of beneficial ownership in procurement.", "question_ids": ["5B.5", "5B.7", "7B.10", "8B.6"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The findings concern the institutional and enforcement status reviewed in 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The report evaluates Mexico's anti-corruption institutions and authorities during AMLO's administration; it does not establish personal enrichment by López Obrador.", "limitations": ["The evidence does not prove that AMLO personally directed corruption or benefited from it."], "start_unit": 88, "end_unit": 92, "locator": "page 88 through page 92", "exact_excerpt": "MEXICO\n\n\n\n     Annex VIII. Leveraging the Anti-Money Laundering Efforts to\n                   Support Anti-Corruption Efforts1\n\nA. Interaction between Mexico’s AML and AC Frameworks\n\n1.      Corruption and money laundering (ML) are symbiotic as they tend to co-occur. The\npresence of one tends to create and reciprocally reinforce the incidence of the other. 2 The strongest\nlink between the two crimes is the need for corruption proceeds to be laundered by the criminals,\nwhich implicates the AML framework. Some of the AML measures—such as the enhanced due\ndiligence measures applicable to Politically Exposed Persons (PEPs) that pose high risk for corruption\nand the filing of Suspicious Transaction Reports (STRs) by reportable entities—could, in turn, be\nhelpful in detecting and preventing corruption in the first place. Furthermore, corruption could\nundermine the proper functioning and effectiveness of the AML/CFT framework (e.g., independence\nand governance of AML/CFT institutions). It is therefore important that frameworks aimed at\ntackling corruption and money laundering work in tandem to tackle financial crimes and limit their\nimpact on the macroeconomy to help ensure a sustainable and inclusive economic growth and\npromote a conducive investment environment.\n\n2.      The 2020 National Risk Assessment on ML and terrorism financing ranked laundering\nof corruption proceeds among the highest risks in Mexico. The preliminary results from the most\nrecent update to the National Risk Assessment confirm that corruption remains among the highest\nML threats. Therefore, it is particularly relevant in the context of Mexico to further refine the\nunderstanding of risks of laundering of domestic corruption to highlight the key sectors at risk and\nthe macroeconomic impact of those crimes. Such understanding of risks of corruption and\nlaundering of its proceeds could help better inform policy determination under the AML and AC\nframeworks and ensure their better integration and complementarity.\n\n3.      The recognition of corruption as a substantial problem in Mexico led to the 2015\nreforms. The National Anti-Corruption System (Sistema Nacional Anticorrupción or SNA) was\nintroduced following amendments to the constitution, along with a package of new laws and legal\namendments that provide for the regulatory and institutional framework in the fight against\ncorruption. 3 The SNA is coordinated by the SNA Coordination Committee comprising seven\n\n1 Prepared by Adrian Wardzynski, with research support from Alessandro D’Amelio (both LEG).\n\n2 Countries that struggle to tackle take decisive actions against corruption usually have lower levels of compliance\n\nwith AML standards. See C. Verdugo, Compliance with the AML/CFT International Standard: Lessons from a Cross-\nCountry Analysis, IMF Working Paper, WP/11/177.\n3 In addition to changes to Article 113 of the Constitution, the AC legal package included the General Law on the\n\nNational Anti-Corruption System (Ley General del Sistema Nacional Anticorrupción), the General Law on\nAdministrative Responsibilities (Ley General de Responsabilidades Administrativas), the Law of Control and\nAccountability of the Federation (Ley de Fiscalización y Rendición de Cuentas de la Federación), amendments to the\nOrganic Law of the Federal Court of Administrative Justice (Ley Orgánica del Tribunal Federal de Justicia\nAdministrativa), amendments to the Organic Law of the Attorney’s General Office (Ley Orgánica de la Fiscalía General\nde la República), amendments to the Federal Penal Code (Código Penal Federal), amendments to the Organic Law of\nFederal Public Administration (Ley Orgánica de la Administración Pública Federal).\n\n\n84   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\nnational institutions. The Financial Intelligence Unit (FIU) and the AML supervisors, i.e., the National\nBanking and Securities Commission (Comisión Nacional Bancaria y de Valores or CNBV) and the tax\nauthority (Servicio de Administración Tributaria or SAT), are indirectly represented at the Committee\nthrough the Ministry of Public Administration (MPA).\n\n4.      Policies of the SNA could reflect better the high risk of laundering of proceeds of\ncorruption. The SNA Coordination Committee approved the National Anti-Corruption Policy\n(Prioridades e implementación de la Política Nacional or PNA) in January 2020 and the National\nImplementation Plan in January 2022. The overall effectiveness of the PNA will also depend on the\nstrength of Mexico’s AML framework, especially given that corruption is among the highest ML\nthreats in Mexico. As part of operationalization of these policies, the priorities for the PNA and its\nimplementation plan benchmarks should reflect AML measures and close coordination with AML\nauthorities.\n\n5.      Coordinating policy priorities for AML and AC could achieve better outcomes. Such\ncoordination could ensure common understanding of risks of corruption and ML and the\nvulnerabilities of different sectors for misuse, better allocation of resources, and prioritization of\nboth frameworks to achieve common outcomes. At the operational level, the MPA, FIU, and AML\nsupervisors should exchange information and cooperate by drawing on the synergies between their\nmandates and relevant information which would enable taking joint action against corruption and\nits proceeds. Given the importance of financial intelligence produced by the FIU to detect and\ndisrupt proceeds of corruption, as well as the role of banks and other financial and non-financial\ngatekeepers in preventing access of “dirty money” into the formal and informal economy, it is\nimportant that the AML and AC policies are coordinated to ensure that the mutually reinforcing\neffects of the AML and AC frameworks are fully utilized.\n\nB. Laundering of Proceeds of Corruption and AML Preventative Measures\n\n6.      PEPs are subject to enhanced due diligence under the AML framework in Mexico. The\nAML financial sector supervisor in Mexico, the CNBV, has dedicated criteria that it uses to verify\nwhether banks and other financial institutions under its supervisory purview are compliant with the\napplicable preventive measures for PEPs. The CNBV has also published guidance to financial entities\non the risks of corruption and related money laundering, which includes details on the necessary\ncontrols related to PEPs. There is also a list of PEPs published by the Ministry of Finance and Public\nCredit to further aid compliance by regulated entities.\n\n7.       The implementation of AML controls applicable to PEPs could be further strengthened\nand the major gaps in the legal framework of the non-financial sector addressed. The CNBV\nfound numerous non-compliance issues in enhanced due diligence processes applied by financial\ninstitutions in relation to PEPs. These include inadequate risk assessment and monitoring of\ntransactions to ensure alignment with the roles, level, and responsibilities of PEPs, failures to\ndetermine PEPs that may be controlling legal entities, reliance on outdated lists of PEPs, as well as\nother documentation and quality of information issues. While these are primarily about aspects of\n\n\n\n                                                                   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND       85\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\neffective implementation, the regulatory framework in the non-financial sector is not yet fully in\nplace. Currently, the Designated Non-Financial Businesses and Professions (DNFBPs)—such as\nlawyers, notaries, accountants, real estate agents, or Trust and Company Service Providers—are not\nlegally obliged under Mexico’s AML legal framework to carry out due diligence checks in relation to\nPEPs. 4 This is particularly important given the key role that such gatekeepers play in laundering\nproceeds of corruption through activities such as running and managing of business activities of\nlegal entities or purchasing of expensive real estate, among the most common techniques to\nlaunder corruption proceeds in Mexico.\n\n8.      STRs filed by the AML regulated entities are an important aspect of detecting financial\ncrimes, including corruption. The FIU indicated that it receives more than 340,000 STRs per year.\nThese cover suspicions of money laundering and its underlying financial crimes such as corruption\nor tax crimes.\n\n9.       Mexico's legal framework for STRs needs significant improvements, especially in\nrelation to DNFBPs. Most of the technical deficiencies in relation to the STRs identified in the\nFinancial Action Task Force (FATF) Mutual Evaluation Report published in 2018 remain unresolved. 5\nIn the case of the STRs obligations applicable to financial institutions, there are issues with the\napplicable timeframes and the level of suspicion required to trigger filing. In the case of the DNFBPs,\nthe existing obligation to file Notices should be amended to ensure comprehensive coverage and\nalignment with the FATF Recommendations. This translates directly into the low levels of reports\nfiled by the DNFBPs and the inability to effectively sanction such practices. More broadly, the overall\nAML regulatory and supervisory framework over the DNFBPs requires substantial strengthening. 6\n\n10.      Mexico should address deficiencies in the implementation of the STR obligations and\nboost the capacity of the FIU to prioritize the STRs related to PEPs. In addition to addressing\nthe legal deficiencies, the implementation of STR obligations should be further strengthened. Based\non the supervisory activities of the CNBV, there are recurrent issues in the accuracy of the customer\ndata that can compromise the integrity and reliability of the client records and related filing\nobligations. Further deficiencies were found by the CNBV in the application of the minimum criteria\nfor the filing of reports under the “24-hours process” and the quality of information included in the\nSTRs (i.e., the description of the underlying operations and the reasons for the suspicions). The FIU\nshould further promote compliance with best practices on the filing of STRs, including those related\nto PEPs and suspicious activities related to laundering generated by corruption, and prioritize\nensuing compliance and raising awareness of the DNBFPs. Given the large number of reports\nreceived by the FIU, the authorities should also ensure that it has adequate resources, both human\nand technological, to carry out sufficient screening of the received reports while prioritizing the STRs\nrelated to PEPs.\n\n4 See the Financial Action Task Force Follow-up Report on Mexico published in May 2023 and available at\n\nhttps://www.fatf-gafi.org/content/dam/fatf-gafi/fur/Follow-Up-Report-Mexico-2023.pdf.coredownload.pdf.\n5 For the 2018 Mutual Evaluation Report, see https://www.fatf-gafi.org/en/publications/Mutualevaluations/Mer-\n\nmexico-2018.html.\n6 See the 2023 Follow-Up Report available at https://www.fatf-gafi.org/content/dam/fatf-gafi/fur/Follow-Up-Report-\n\nMexico-2023.pdf.coredownload.pdf.\n\n\n86   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\n11.      Misuse of legal entities is among the techniques most used in Mexico to launder\nproceeds of crimes, including corruption. Shell companies and front companies are commonly\nutilized to conceal the beneficial ownership of assets and funds as well as the true purpose of\ntransactions. These are also used by PEPs in Mexico to cover payment of bribes and embezzle public\nfunds.\n\n12.      Implementation of the AML framework in Mexico does not adequately ensure\navailability of beneficial ownership information. The AML framework requires that regulated\nentities identify beneficial ownership information as part of their customer due diligence processes.\nThere can be however doubts about the veracity of this source of beneficial ownership information\nin light of the effectiveness issues. 7 As identified by the CNBV, there are significant issues in the\nprocedures followed by financial institutions to identify beneficial owners including for PEPs.\nDeficiencies have been identified in the collection and review of the necessary data and\ndocumentation that is essential for this largely factual test. The difficulties in reliance on the\nbeneficial ownership information obtained through regulated entities were also acknowledged by\nthe FIU that requires it for the purposes of financial analysis and production of financial intelligence\nreports for law enforcement agencies. The prosecutors generally rely on their own analysis—\nincluding review of the legal structure, board minutes, and field-based investigations—for the\ndetermination of beneficial ownership information. Given the key value of beneficial ownership\ninformation in tackling financial crimes, the authorities should step up supervisory oversight and\napply proportional and dissuasive sanctions in cases of non-compliance. There are also significant\nissues in the legal framework applicable to the DNFBPs, including in relation to the understanding of\nthe legal and control structure of the customers, which should be addressed through legislative\nchanges.\n\n13.     The new beneficial ownership information requirements arising under the tax laws are\ncurrently underutilized for AML purposes. The amendments to the Federal Tax Code in\nNovember 2021, which took effect in January 2022, introduced new obligations pursuant to Article\n32-B Ter on legal entities and their service providers to obtain and report beneficial ownership\ninformation to the Mexican tax authorities (SAT). These new rules were introduced to enable the SAT\nto obtain such information upon request and then report to foreign tax authorities in compliance\nwith requirements arising under the international tax transparency standards overseen by the Global\nForum on Transparency and Exchange of Information. 8 While these may also be of value for the\nbroader entity transparency objectives and tackling of financial crimes, they have not been used to\ndate in this context. Moreover, given the specific rationale for the introduction of these new\nobligations, there is no specific database that can be consulted by other competent authorities and\nregulated entities at the SAT for the beneficial ownership information that it has the ability to\nrequest. The SAT also does not verify the information. Mexico should introduce a beneficial\n\n7 For an analysis regarding the overall low effectiveness of the AML preventive measures, including those relating to\n\nbeneficial ownership requirements, see the 2018 Mutual Evaluation Report of Mexico adopted by the FATF and\navailable at https://www.fatf-gafi.org/en/countries/detail/Mexico.html.\n8 See https://www.oecd.org/publications/global-forum-on-transparency-and-exchange-of-information-for-tax-\n\npurposes-mexico-2023-second-round-6fd9ab78-en.htm.\n\n\n\n                                                                            INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND            87\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\nownership registry and establish relevant processes to ensure that the information collected is\naccurate, adequate, and up to date. Beneficial ownership information should also be required to be\ncollected and verified as part of the public procurement process.\n\nC. Prosecution of Money Laundering and Corruption\n\n14.      The jurisdiction and cooperation between the specialized prosecutor offices\nresponsible for money laundering and corruption in Mexico should be enhanced. Money\nlaundering cases are prosecuted by the Specialized Prosecutor Office for Organized Crime (Fiscalía\nEspecializada en Materia de Delincuencia Organizada or FEMDO). This office can also carry out\nparallel investigations of money laundering and other financial crimes, such as corruption, if they are\nconsidered of high impact. In 2019 the Specialized Prosecutor Office for Corruption (Fiscalía\nEspecializada en Combate a la Corrupción or FMCCO) was established. While this office was\nexpected to take the lead on corruption-related cases, it has mainly dealt with matters relating to\nabuse of office, which is one of the forms of corruption. Corruption and bribery cases are not among\nits top five categories of cases and it has not yet prosecuted any high-level corruption cases. The\nauthorities should clarify the jurisdiction between the FEMDO and FEMCCO and also provide for\ntheir close cooperation to avoid fragmentation that would adversely affect the effectiveness to\nprosecute corruption cases and related money laundering.\n\n15.     The authorities should ensure that there are strict AC policies applicable to the law\nenforcement authorities, at both federal and state levels, as well as the judiciary. The level of\ncorruption in Mexico can affect the functioning of the law enforcement system and undermines the\ncapacity to investigate and prosecute corruption and money laundering. The number prosecutions\nand convictions relating to corruption and money laundering is not commensurate with Mexico’s\nrisk profile. The authorities should take steps to increase resources, capacity, and expertise as well as\nensure adequate budget of the law enforcement authorities. Confiscation and asset recovery should\nbe seen as major policy objectives within the national AML and AC policies and implementation\nplans in order to ensure that crime does not pay.\n\n\n\n\n88   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND", "excerpt_sha256": "cbaeabc12eb75d780c6f0f351a804d89cb38d728fd290435b2a3739b815d8dca", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0016-E022", "source_id": "SRC-684988a395d1a128", "url": "https://www.banxico.org.mx/publications-and-press/imf-article-iv-consultation-for-mexico/%7B2B9230B7-CC63-5B20-E173-F5E94D116BC9%7D.pdf", "title": "IMF Country Report No. 23/356 — Mexico", "publisher": "Banco de México / International Monetary Fund", "raw_sha256": "f93592f437a264c95c4c753720221642f0dd636d55e012cef97b95d1b59a294a", "fact_summary": "The IMF found limited foreign-bribery risk but reported no completed foreign-bribery prosecution, inadequate whistleblower protection, unclear corporate liability, and unresolved deficiencies in PEP monitoring, beneficial-ownership identification, suspicious-transaction reporting, and DNFBP supervision. It acknowledged legal and institutional reforms and broader PEP controls while calling for effective enforcement.", "question_ids": ["5B.5", "5B.7", "7B.10", "8B.6"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The supplementary information updates the assessment as of October 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The findings concern Mexican authorities and institutions under AMLO; they do not establish personal conduct by López Obrador.", "limitations": ["The source explicitly notes that some information supplied by Mexico was not independently verified by the OECD monitoring body."], "start_unit": 103, "end_unit": 105, "locator": "page 103 through page 105", "exact_excerpt": "MEXICO\n\n\n\nTRANSNATIONAL ASPECTS OF CORRUPTION1\nA. Introduction 2\n1.       Foreign bribery risks are relatively limited but given the export-driven nature of the\neconomy, further mitigating efforts are called for. Among the 500 largest multinational\nenterprises (MNEs) in the world, only one multinational enterprise (MNE) is headquartered in\nMexico. It is also not among the major sources of foreign direct investment to the rest of the world. 3\nMexico has engaged in anti-bribery institutional and legal reforms and enhanced awareness of\npublic officials to report foreign bribery. However, there has been no prosecution of foreign bribery\ncases. It is advisable for the authorities to take further measures, including to ensure adequate\nprotection of reporting persons and promote effective enforcement.\n\n2.        While risks of laundering of proceeds of foreign corruption in Mexico are overall\nlimited, they should be further mitigated. There is no indication of major threats of laundering of\nproceeds of foreign corruption in Mexico and the AML framework already includes several\nmitigating measures to detect and disrupt such proceeds including preventive measures in the\nfinancial sector focused on politically exposed persons (PEPs). This could be further enhanced by\nfacilitating reporting of suspicious activities from the key gatekeepers, including in the non-financial\nsector (e.g., real estate agents, lawyers, accountants), which should also cover suspicions in relation\nto PEPs. The latter would require introducing the relevant regulatory into the preventive and\nsupervisory framework for the non-financial sector, which should be seen as a priority. The\nauthorities should further continue improving the availability, accuracy, and access to beneficial\nownership information of companies to prevent their misuse for the laundering of foreign proceeds\nof corruption. 4\n\nB. Supply Side of Corruption 5\n3.    Mexico has taken steps to enhance its anti-bribery legal and institutional framework,\nthough more efforts are needed to address foreign bribery. It has engaged in important\n\n1 Prepared by Yao Deng and Adrian Wardzynski (both LEG).\n\n2 Mexico volunteered to have its legal and institutional frameworks assessed in the context of bilateral surveillance\n\nfor purposes of determining whether it: (a) criminalizes and prosecutes the bribery of foreign public officials; and (b)\nhas an effective AML/CFT system that is designed to prevent foreign officials from concealing the proceeds of\ncorruption.\n3 See OECD- UNSD Multinational Enterprise Information Platform, and International Financial Statistics International\n\nInvestment Position, Assets, Direct investment.\n4 For an analysis regarding the overall low effectiveness of the preventive measures set out in the AML framework see\n\nthe 2018 Mutual Evaluation Report of Mexico adopted by the FATF and available at https://www.fatf-\ngafi.org/en/countries/detail/Mexico.html.\n5 Information relating to supply-side corruption in this annex draws on the WGB’s Phase 4 Report of Mexico (October\n\n2018), the follow-up report (August 2022, updated) and the additional written report (June 2023). The IMF staff and\nMexico have provided additional views and information whose accuracy have not been verified by the WGB or the\nOECD Secretariat, and which do not prejudice the WGB’s monitoring of the implementation of the OECD Anti-Bribery\nConvention.\n\n\n2    INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\ninstitutional and legal reforms in the fight against foreign bribery, including amending the foreign\nbribery offense to be applicable to cases involving third party beneficiaries, making corporate\nliability for foreign bribery independent of the liability of the individual perpetrators, increasing the\nmaximum sanctions for accounting offenses, and making bribes non-tax-deductible. In addition, it\nhas initiated judicial statistical collection aiming to provide comprehensive information about\nforeign bribery enforcement and introduced successor liability into the criminal corporate liability\nframework. Mexico further took efforts to raise awareness of public officials of their obligation to\nreport instances of foreign bribery through the provision of training. While acknowledging this\nprogress, the 2022 follow-up report of the OECD Working Group on Bribery in International Business\nTransactions (WGB) expressed concerns over the adequacy of Mexico’s efforts to address foreign\nbribery, especially given the export driven nature of the Mexican economy and the inclusion of high-\nrisk sectors for corruption in exports, such as extractives, manufacturing, and agricultural products.\n\n4.      Looking forward, Mexico is encouraged to take further measures, inter alia, to ensure\nadequate whistleblower protection, clarify liability of legal persons, and enhance effective\nenforcement against foreign bribery. A specific law providing for whistleblower protection of\npublic and private sector employees that report suspicions of foreign bribery has not been enacted,\nthough a draft bill was reported as being under discussion. 6 In addition, obstacles to reporting by\ngovernment agencies remain; for example, the tax authorities have difficulties in reporting or\nsharing suspicions of foreign bribery. While the liability of legal persons for foreign bribery is not\nclear under the current legal framework and the coverage of foreign bribery cases by the General\nLaw on Administrative Responsibility needs to be further confirmed, the State-Owned Enterprises\ncannot be held liable for foreign bribery. 7 Most notably, however, Mexico is yet to successfully\nconclude its first foreign bribery case. The lack of proactive investigative measures in foreign bribery\ninvestigations poses challenges. According to the data available, there were only a few foreign\nbribery investigations in Mexico which did not progress to the prosecution stage. It will be\nimportant to take stock of the impact of the nomination of a Specialized Prosecutor for Combatting\nCorruption (FECC) since 2019 on the investigation and prosecution of foreign bribery. The\nauthorities should take additional steps to prioritize detection, enforcement, and effective\nsanctioning of foreign bribery cases in line with the OECD WGB’s Phase 4 recommendations.\n\nC. Facilitation of Proceeds of Foreign Corruption (Concealment-Side)\n\n5.      Mexico’s AML regime helps prevent laundering of foreign proceeds of corruption. The\n2020 National Risk Assessment (NRA) carried out by Mexico recognized corruption, including\ntransnational corruption, as a structural risk. Specific guidance was subsequently released on\ncorruption risks along with several related typologies. The definition of Politically Exposed Persons\n(PEPs), which includes domestic and foreign PEPs, was further broadened following the 2018 Mutual\n\n6 The discussion on the draft was reported by Mexico in its 2023 Additional Written Report.\n\n7 In the Additional Written Report submitted to the OECD Working Group on Bribery in June 2023, Mexico stated\n\nthat the criminal and administrative liabilities of legal persons belong to two different areas. Therefore, article 25 of\nthe General Law of Administrative Responsibilities (LGRA) cannot be applied in the context of a criminal proceeding\nthat involves a legal entity pursuant to article 421 of the National Code of Criminal Procedures.\n\n\n\n                                                                               INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND              3\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\nEvaluation Report (MER) of Mexico to ensure its alignment with the FATF Recommendations and the\nMinistry of Finance and Public Credit issues a list of public officials that are considered PEPs. 8 The\nPEP provisions provide for enhanced due diligence, including close monitoring and possibility of\nverification of the source of funds and wealth. The CNBV has specific inspection criteria relating to\ncorruption and beneficial ownership customer due diligence obligations imposed on certain\nfinancial institutions. The CNBV and Financial Intelligence Unit (FIU) also published guidelines on\nprevention and detection of corruption, including the risks and specific requirements relating to\nPEPs. Recently, there were further improvements in the Federal Tax Code relating to the availability\nof beneficial ownership information in Mexico that carry the potential to enhance the capacity of the\nauthorities to prevent misuse of legal vehicles in concealing the ownership of illicit assets and\nproceeds, including those stemming from corruption. Mexico has also updated its NRA to expand its\nunderstanding of risks regarding the potential for misuse of the different types of legal persons\nbased on complaints, criminal investigations, prosecutions, and convictions, as well as transnational\nrequests.\n\n6.        The authorities should continue their efforts to prevent laundering of foreign\nproceeds of corruption by bolstering the applicability of the relevant AML measures. As part\nof its risk-based supervision carried out by the CNBV identified irregularities in the application of the\nenhanced due diligence applicable to PEPs, several issues with the effective implementation of the\nenhanced due diligence with respect to PEPs were identified. These include issues with effective\nmonitoring of PEPs in line with their risk profile as well as issues with the identification and\nobtaining of information on PEPs that are beneficial owners. As far as beneficial ownership is\nconcerned, significant deficiencies have also been found by the CNBV in the development of\nprocedures and identification of beneficial owners by financial institutions, including collection of\nthe necessary data and documentation. The CNBV further found that the STRs submitted by\nfinancial institutions do not always include all the relevant fields and improperly implement best\npractices issued by the FIU. The obligations regarding filing of Notices by the DNFBPs need to be\namended to ensure compliance with the FATF Recommendations, particularly FATF\nRecommendation 23, and thus the relevant legal reform pending in the Mexican Congress should be\napproved and enacted without any further delays. 9 In the same vein, the authorities should, as a\nmatter of priority, introduce the regulatory and supervisory framework over the DNFBPs in line with\nthe risk-based approach mandated by the international standards. As part of its risk-based\nsupervisory priorities, the CNBV should put greater emphasis on the risk of misuse of legal entities\nto conceal payment of bribes and cross-border transactions relating to possible corruption\nproceeds. The authorities should pursue parallel money laundering, corruption, and related\norganized crimes investigations and prosecutions including when corruption is committed abroad,\nand cases of non-compliance should be subject to effective sanctions mechanisms. The available\ninternational exchange mechanisms should be utilized to a greater extent in detecting and\ninvestigating foreign bribery.\n\n8 For the 2018 Mutual Evaluation Report of Mexico adopted by the FATF and the subsequent 2021, 2022, and 2023\n\nFollow-Up Reports see https://www.fatf-gafi.org/en/countries/detail/Mexico.html.\n9 See FATF Follow-Up Report from May 2023 available at https://www.fatf-gafi.org/en/countries/detail/Mexico.html.\n\n\n\n\n4    INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND", "excerpt_sha256": "5933e6b99d53603ed8683d321fa435f9d8613a8bb9a8ce3a75d2362126874f81", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0017-E001", "source_id": "SRC-065783efb0b09db2", "url": "https://www.banxico.org.mx/publications-and-press/imf-article-iv-consultation-for-mexico/%7B44F43B6B-61BD-D795-4561-1601FDD3FDB0%7D.pdf", "title": "Mexico: 2022 Article IV Consultation—Press Release and Staff Report", "publisher": "International Monetary Fund", "raw_sha256": "8fac4146590dbe2ed458f7356748f5880d021e03cc4982b1e7efe083d33f622a", "fact_summary": "The Mexican authorities used largely untargeted subsidies to mitigate cost-of-living pressures. Headline inflation rose from about 3 percent at the end-2020 to 7.4\npercent by end-2021 and to 8.7 percent by August this year. Economic activity is expected to\nslow from 2.1 percent in 2022 to 1.2 percent in 2023, given weaker U.S. and global growth\nand tighter global financial conditions. Inflation is projected to plateau in the second half of\n2022 and then gradually decline.\n\nThe government has maintained a broadly neutral fiscal stance in 2022, targeting a fiscal\ndeficit of 3.8 percent of GDP. It has used largely untargeted subsidies to mitigate the rise in\nthe cost of living, offsetting the windfall gains from oil revenues. The gross debt of the public\nsector (by staff’s definition) is estimated at about 56 percent of GDP. With inflation above\ntarget, the central bank has increased the policy rate to 9.25 percent in a series of hikes.\nReflecting more resilient domestic demand growth compared to output, the current account\nposted a deficit of 0.4 percent of GDP in 2021 and is expected to widen to around 1 percent of\nGDP in 2022-2023. International reserves remain at a comfortable level.\n\nThe Mexican banking system remains well-capitalized, and its profitability continues to recover\nfrom the impact of the pandemic. Risk analysis as part of the Financial Sector Assessment\nProgram (FSAP), conducted in parallel with the Article IV consultation, found that the systemic\nvulnerabilities and system-wide liquidity risks in the financial system remain broadly contained,\ngiven high capital buffers, low private sector leverage, and no sign of stretched asset prices.\nHowever, tail risks bear close monitoring. Financial sector policies have been strengthened in\nrecent years, but further steps are necessary to maintain the resilience of the financial system\nunder the evolving risk environment.\n\nExecutive Board Assessment2\n\nExecutive Directors agreed with the thrust of the staff appraisal. They welcomed the\nauthorities’ prudent macroeconomic policies in the face of a surge in inflation. They noted that\neconomic growth is expected to slow in the near term reflecting weaker U.S. growth and\ntighter global financial conditions. With increased risks in a more turbulent global environment,\n\n\n\n1 Under Article IV of the IMF's Articles of Agreement, the IMF holds bilateral discussions with members, usually every year. A staff\nteam visits the country, collects economic and financial information, and discusses with officials the country's economic developments\nand policies. On return to headquarters, the staff prepares a report, which forms the basis for discussion by the Executive Board.\n2 At the conclusion of the discussion, the Managing Director, as Chairman of the Board, summarizes the views of Executive Directors,\nand this summary is transmitted to the country's authorities. An explanation of any qualifiers used in summings up can be found here:\nhttp://www.IMF.org/external/np/sec/misc/qualifiers.htm.\n\n2\n\n\nDirectors agreed that Mexico is well placed to navigate the challenges, owing to its very strong\nmacroeconomic policies and policy frameworks.\n\nDirectors welcomed Banco de México’s proactive approach to addressing the inflation surge.\nWith significant uncertainty about the inflation path in 2023 and upside risks, they concurred\nthat further increases in the policy rate and maintaining a restrictive policy stance may be\nneeded for some time. They also generally underscored the importance of clear policy\ncommunication. Directors recommended aligning wage increases more closely with expected\ninflation and the productivity of lower wage workers.\n\nDirectors agreed that the envisaged neutral fiscal stance in 2022 and 2023 is appropriate.\nThey noted that retail fuel price smoothing, while reducing cost pressures, had a sizable cost\non the budget, and generally encouraged the authorities to shift toward more targeted support\nshould oil prices rise again in order to protect other priority spending. Contingency plans\nshould be prepared for a swift policy response should downside risks materialize. Increasing\nfiscal buffers in the short term and reforming the institutional framework for greater flexibility in\nthe longer term would be important. Directors noted that the floating exchange rate should\ncontinue to act as a shock absorber, although foreign exchange intervention could be\nconsidered if there is a significant worsening of market illiquidity.\n\nDirectors agreed that the financial system remains resilient post pandemic, welcomed the\nfavorable assessment of the effectiveness of Mexico’s financial stability framework, and\nbroadly supported the key policy recommendations of the 2022 Financial Sector Assessment\nProgram. They concurred that additional measures, including an update of the financial sector\noversight and crisis management frameworks, are needed to keep up with a changing risk and\nregulatory landscape. After aligning the legal and regulatory AML/CFT framework with\nFinancial Action Task Force standards, the focus should now be to strengthen the\neffectiveness of the AML/CFT regime, including through adequate resources, ensuring\navailability of high-quality beneficial ownership information, and monitoring emerging financial\nrisks related to fintech.\n\nDirectors urged the authorities to broaden the supply side policy agenda for higher and more\ninclusive growth. Efforts should include measures to address corruption and crime and\nstrengthen the rule of law, increase human capital investment, tackle infrastructure\nbottlenecks, reduce labor and product market rigidities, and promote financial deepening and\ninclusion. Directors recommended a gradual increase in productive spending, financed by tax\nreforms, to improve human and physical capital.\n\nDirectors generally noted the need to take further steps toward reducing greenhouse\nemissions. Fostering greater private sector participation in the energy sector would help boost\ncompetitiveness and investment.\n\nIt is expected that the next Article IV consultation with Mexico will be held on the standard 12-\nmonth cycle.", "excerpt_sha256": "cbe620a4c52802d4d0990b77e14d0884d669e805b8c7969ff431d01436d0f522", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt supports largely untargeted subsidies and the fiscal-cost/targeting recommendation.", "The excerpt does not support attributing disproportionate benefits to higher-income households; Tackling High Inflation\n\nMonetary Policy\n\n19.      Banxico has taken a proactive approach to address increasingly broad-based inflation.\nSince June 2021, successive and gradually larger increases in the reference rate brought the ex ante\nreal policy rate (i.e., the nominal rate adjusted for one-year ahead inflation expectations) to a\nmoderately restrictive level by September 2022 at about 4 percent. 6 This has been an appropriately\ncalibrated response to the upward surprises to inflation and sequential delays in returning to the\ninflation target. In recent monetary policy statements, Banxico has also rightly indicated its intention\nto further increase the policy rate. It is expected that a policy rate of at least 10 percent would be\nrequired for inflation to reverse close to the central bank’s 3-percent target within the usual two\nyears or so.\n\n\n                   Inflation and Expectations\n                                                                        Real Ex-Ante Policy Rates\n                    (Seasonally adjusted, y/y)\n         9                                                                      (Percent)\n                                                                 10\n                                                                        Brazil\n                                                  Headline\n         8                                        Inflation      8      Chile\n                                                                        Mexico\n         7                                        Core           6      Peru\n                                                  Inflation\n                                                                        Colombia\n                                                                 4\n         6                                        Expectations\n                                                  : 12 Months    2\n         5                                        Expectations\n                                                                 0\n                                                  : 1-4 Years\n         4                                                       -2\n                                                  Expectations\n                                                  : 5-8 Years\n         3                                                       -4\n\n\n\n\n        Sources: Banxico and Haver Analytics.\n\n\n\n20.      Inflation risks are expected to remain heightened for some time. The proactive policy\ntightening already put in place, alongside further increases in the policy rate, and weakening global\ndemand are expected to lead to a slowdown in activity and a decline in inflation. However, there is\nsignificant uncertainty about the timing, speed, and durability of the downward path for inflation.\nThis uncertainty arises from the possibility of: (i) further shocks, including spillovers to local raw and\nprocessed food prices from global commodity prices and domestic food prices, especially resulting\nfrom severe drought conditions in some Mexican states; (ii) if inflation imported from advanced\neconomies is more persistent than expected; (iii) longer-lasting price pressures from supply chain\n\n\n6 In its April–June 2019 Quarterly Report, Banxico used a range of models to establish a range between 1.8 and 3.4\n\npercent for the neutral real policy rate.\n\n\n\n                                                                           INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND           13\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\nconstraints; (iv) further expected increases in the minimum wage feeding into costs and prices;\n(v) inertia resulting from more frequent domestic price adjustments; and (vi) further upticks in near-\nterm inflation expectations that then feed in to wages and prices. The inflation surge in Mexico\nreflects both shocks in standard determinants (such as relative food and import prices, falling slack,\nand rising inflation expectations), but also a series of positive residual factors, and preliminary signs\nof greater inertia (Annex IX).\n\n\n                Inflation Expectations—1-4 Years Ahead                           Mexico Processed Food and U.S. Overall Food\n                      (Central Bank survey responses)                                             Inflation\n     6.0                                                              0.5   14\n                                                                                                              Mex - Core Food\n                                                                            12\n                                                                      0.4                                     U.S. - Food & Bev\n     5.0\n                                                                            10\n                                                                      0.3\n                                                                             8\n     4.0\n                                                                      0.2    6\n\n     3.0                                                                     4\n                                                                      0.1\n                                                                             2\n     2.0                                                              0.0\n                                                                             0\n\n                                                                            -2\n              Interquartile Range        Total Range\n              Average                    Interquartile Spread (RHS)\n\n\n Sources: Haver Analytics and Banxico.\n\n\n\n21.      Risk considerations call for further increases in the policy rate and maintaining it at\nthat higher level. The risks to inflation are not only high but also asymmetric in the usual one-to-\ntwo-year monetary policy horizon: upside surprises to inflation seem more likely than downside\nshocks. One contributing factor is backward-looking elements in the inflation process in Mexico\naccording to Philips curve estimates for Mexico (Annex IX), which suggests additional risks from\nrecent inflation shocks. Given these asymmetric risks and the substantial costs of de-anchoring wage\nand price formation, a risk management approach would argue for policy rates to rise further and\nstay firmly restrictive for some time, so as to keep wage and price formation anchored. Such an\napproach would entail a real ex ante policy rate that would peak at over 5 percent in 2023, broadly\nconsistent with market expectations for nominal rates and staff’s inflation forecast.\n\n22.     Clear monetary policy communication will increase policy effectiveness during this\nperiod of significant inflation uncertainty. Banxico has appropriately taken steps to help the\npublic better understand its policy decisions in this context. Steps have included the publication of\nupdated inflation forecasts with every rate decision since August 2021 and, more recently, guidance\non the direction of the next policy rate change in the monetary policy statement. These efforts have\nhelped the public better understand the Governing Board’s policy decision. To further strengthen\nthis understanding, Banxico could begin publishing more information on the policy rate path that\nunderpins its macro forecast, including the expected rate and length of its stay at the peak of the\ntightening cycle, and possible triggers for changes in policies. If clearly presented as expectations\nand not a policy commitment, changes in the expected policy rate path would provide valuable\ninformation on how the central bank expects to adjust policy as economic conditions change. A\nbroader review of the experience with Banxico’s inflation targeting framework over the past two\n\n\n\n14         INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\ndecades could provide useful suggestions for further improvements to the policy framework and the\ncommunications toolkit.\n\nFiscal Measures\n\n23.      The authorities have used largely untargeted subsidies to mitigate the rise in the cost\nof living. The authorities expect that these subsidies will cost around 2 percent of GDP (see Annex\nVI for a description of the authorities’ anti-inflationary fiscal measures) which include:\n\n•   Retail fuel price stabilization has directly reduced cost pressures, lowering inflation by an\n    estimated 2 percentage points this year. The measure has come at a sizeable budgetary cost,\n    estimated at around 1.5 percent of GDP, and has disproportionately benefited higher income\n    households. Furthermore, by diluting price signals, the policy has short-circuited the desirable\n    adjustment in fuel demand.\n\n•   The measures to mitigate the impact of higher food prices have come at a smaller budgetary\n    cost. They have mostly relied on preexisting programs (e.g., increased emphasis on direct\n    fertilizer provision to small farmers or support for subsistence farming), and their direct impact\n    on inflation has been minimal.\n\n24.      Overall, these fiscal and preexisting redistributive measures have helped support real\nincomes. The higher food and energy prices have only increased the deficit by around ¼ percent of\nGDP with fuel subsidies offset by higher oil revenues and food-related measures funded from\nreducing other expenditures. However, in the event of a renewed increase in energy prices, the fiscal\neffects would depend on the level of refining margins (“crack spread”) and the duration of the\nhigher global prices. In addition to these efforts to lessen the increase in the cost of living, the\nadministration has increased the universal social (noncontributory) pensions and is projected to\nraise the minimum wage from 42 percent of the median formal sector wage in 2018 to 59 percent in\n2022). Overall, in the context of a stronger economic recovery and record-high remittance inflow,\nthese fiscal efforts, the higher minimum wage, and other labor market measures may have\ncontributed to an increase in real per capita labor income of about 5 percent (y/y) by mid-2022.\n\n25.      The proposed neutral fiscal stance in 2023 strikes a balance between supporting\nmonetary policy in its disinflation efforts while avoiding a material fiscal drag on activity.\nWith the economy currently operating at close to potential the priority is to restore low and stable\ninflation. A restrictive monetary stance and a broadly neutral fiscal stance provide an appropriate\npolicy mix to achieve this goal. However, continuing large minimum wage increases will likely add to\ninflation pressures, working at counter purpose to monetary and fiscal policies and potentially\nreducing formal employment for lower income workers (see below).\n\nAuthorities’ Views\n\n26.    The authorities agreed with the need for a robust monetary policy to keep inflation\nexpectations well-anchored. The early and decisive monetary policy response to the global\n\n\n\n                                                                   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND       15\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\ninflation surge had been successful, noting that upside risks to inflation were recognized early even\nwhen the surge was still considered transitory in the baseline. They underscored that the real policy\nrate was now in restrictive territory despite continued upward surprises to inflation. The authorities\nhighlighted their strengthened communication efforts, which had ensured smooth financial market\nadjustment in this hiking cycle. They will continue to evaluate other tools that could improve\ncommunication.\n\n27.      The anti-inflationary fiscal measures have helped to mitigate the effects of inflation on\nthose most exposed. The authorities emphasized that, despite these measures, they sustained\nmomentum on the administration’s flagship reforms. Fuel price stabilization in particular had been\ncentral to efforts to support households and restrain inflation, in line with the President’s\ncommitment to the people. They highlighted analysis suggesting that, in addition to supporting\nhouseholds, the pricing mechanism has dampening second-round inflation effects. Other anti-\ninflation measures had largely leveraged existing policies, especially in agriculture and for basic\nnecessities other than fuels. The authorities underscored that the main goal of these policies was to\nsupport historically underserved agricultural communities, although they also expected positive\neffects on agricultural yields.\n\nB. Managing Potential Downside Risks\n\nFiscal Policy\n\n28.     Fiscal policy could better prepare for downside risks. The neutral fiscal stance could be\nmaintained if growth were to slow modestly. However, more could be done to be ready to provide\ntargeted support for poorer households. In the event of a significant weakening of activity,\nadditional discretionary fiscal support should be considered.\n\n29.    Under Mexico’s fiscal framework—which includes both a balanced budget rule and\nconstraints on debt issuance—the scope to pursue a countercyclical fiscal policy is limited.\nModest steps could, however, increase the ability of fiscal policy to provide targeted support in the\nevent of a negative shock.\n\n•    Changing the retail fuel price regime could create room for more targeted support in case\n     of a prolonged global oil price increase. More market-based fuel pricing could allow for a\n     passthrough of global fuel prices to domestic retail prices, encourage transition to greener\n     sources of energy, and reduce the budgetary cost of subsidies. This would also strengthen the\n     price signals for fuel demand and allow the fiscal savings to be, at least partially, reinvested in\n     targeted support for vulnerable households 7 by leveraging existing social safety nets (see Fiscal\n     Policy for Mitigating the Social Impact of High Energy and Food Prices, IMF 2022) or relying on\n\n7 The fuel tax expenditure under the current regime entails large leakage of support to higher income households.\n\nUsing 2020 data, the authorities estimate that the top 20 percent of the income distribution accounted for about 45\npercent of the fuel excise tax collection whereas the bottom 20 percent accounted for 6 percent (Distribución del\npago de impuestos y recepción del gasto público por deciles de hogares y personas. Resultados para el año 2020,\nMinistry of Finance). Broad-based reductions in tax rates are therefore likely to entail disproportionately large gains\nfor richer households.\n\n\n16    INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND", "excerpt_sha256": "947592e6ac6b78daff5a5827380af57b6c1edf17bf36fddbad6e2a1f49a252f8", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt supports successive, gradually larger rate increases; an appropriately calibrated response; a restrictive stance; strengthened communication; and significant uncertainty with upside inflation risks."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0017-E003", "source_id": "SRC-065783efb0b09db2", "url": "https://www.banxico.org.mx/publications-and-press/imf-article-iv-consultation-for-mexico/%7B44F43B6B-61BD-D795-4561-1601FDD3FDB0%7D.pdf", "title": "Mexico: 2022 Article IV Consultation—Press Release and Staff Report", "publisher": "International Monetary Fund", "raw_sha256": "8fac4146590dbe2ed458f7356748f5880d021e03cc4982b1e7efe083d33f622a", "fact_summary": "Fiscal reserves in Mexico’s stabilization fund had fallen below 0.1 percent of GDP after extensive pandemic use. Proposed amendments connected to the 2023 budget would use headroom under the government’s debt ceiling to bring reserves closer to the authorities’ desired 0.3 percent of GDP, enhancing short-term preparedness; IMF staff said more extensive medium-term reforms were needed.", "question_ids": ["5B.3", "5B.9", "6B.6", "8B.2"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The source directly evaluates reforms proposed for the 2023 budget and their preparedness for 2023 shocks.", "ruler_attribution": "The reforms are attributed to the Mexican government/authorities under AMLO's administration.", "limitations": ["The account concerns proposed or expected reforms at the time of the 2022 consultation; subsequent enactment and operation are not documented.", "The source gives institutional analysis but not AMLO's personal rationale."], "start_unit": 21, "end_unit": 22, "locator": "page 21 through page 22", "exact_excerpt": "MEXICO\n\n\n\n    other tools (such as one-off cash payments, temporary energy bill discounts, or temporary\n    public transport subsidies).\n\n•   Implementing, and building upon, proposed reforms to the fiscal framework. Fiscal reserves\n    in the Fondo de Estabilización de los Ingresos Presupuestarios (FEIP, also known as ‘the\n    stabilization fund’) have fallen to less than 0.1 percent of GDP, given extensive usage during the\n    pandemic and a restrictive replenishment mechanism under the Federal Budget and Fiscal\n    Responsibility Law (FRBL). 8 This is a potential constraint on the government’s ability to respond\n    quickly to future shocks. The authorities estimate that a minimum of 0.3 percent of GDP would\n    provide sufficient resources for a fiscal policy response. Recent amendments to the FRBL\n    proposed by the authorities as part of the 2023 budget would bring FEIP reserves closer to this\n    desired level, by enabling utilization of headroom against the government’s debt ceiling. This\n    approach would enhance preparedness in the short-term. However, in the medium term, more\n    extensive reforms are required to enhance flexibility more permanently, while also ensuring\n    sustainability and credibility over the medium-term (Annex X). Such reforms could include\n    (Annex IV of Staff Report 2019): (i) a well-calibrated debt anchor; (ii) broader coverage of\n    expenditure in the structural spending rule; (iii) a medium-term fiscal strategy that specifies the\n    post-shock adjustment path to return to the debt target; (iv) tighter triggers for the use of\n    escape clauses; and (v) a strengthened role for the fiscal council.\n\n30.     A medium-term fiscal strategy could help to anchor expectations about future fiscal\npolicy. The public debt ratio is expected to remain broadly unchanged in the next decade. 9 Weaker-\nthan-expected growth or adjustment slippages would thus result in a rising debt path. Nonetheless,\nthe Sovereign Risk and Debt Sustainability Analysis (SRDSA) (Annex III) indicates that public debt\nremains sustainable. With the proposed fiscal framework reform, a credible debt anchor and a\nmedium-term fiscal strategy could increase the scope to let fiscal policy act as a shock absorber and\nreduce the volatility in activity as a result.\n\nAuthorities’ Views\n\n31.     The authorities see advantages to maintaining the current retail fuel price stabilization\nmechanism. Given the current administration’s pledge that fuel prices will not rise in real terms\nabove their November 2018 level and the legal basis of the pricing mechanism, the authorities\nprefer to continue with the current prompt and programmatic fuel price stabilization mechanism\nrather than more targeted support.\n\n8 Under Mexico’s fiscal framework, FEIP has served as the de facto source of contingent financing for the budget\n\nalongside smaller trust funds when revenue shortfalls materialized during the 2020 downturn. These fiscal reserves\nare critical as Mexico’s fiscal framework does not permit the issuance of debt for non-capital spending. The current\nframework, enshrined in the FBFRL, also places restrictions on how FEIP can be replenished, with balance sheet\ntransfers to FEIP likely viewed as expenditures that must be balanced by a pre-identified revenue source or spending\ncuts elsewhere in the budget.\n9 The growth-adjusted interest factor, sometimes referred to as (r-g), captures the interest costs of the debt ratio,\n\nadjusted for growth effects, which, on their own, lower the latter. Staff’s baseline projections for the SRDSA include\nincreasing fiscal gaps in the outer years, which reflect the estimated adjustment needed to achieve the deficits\nprojected in the authorities’ latest Budget Update.\n\n\n                                                                              INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND               17\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\nWith oil prices expected to decline next year, the formula underpinning the fuel pricing mechanism\nis expected to result in lower subsidies in 2023. The authorities pointed to a track record of tapering\nsubsidies when market price pressures abated. Other policies addressing food prices include the\nremoval of tariffs on key food imports and regulatory agreements with retailers on food price\npassthrough. However, in anticipation of possible downside risks, they are interested in reforms to\nthe fiscal framework that would enable the allocation of fiscal savings and funding from debt\nissuance to the stabilization fund, while respecting key tenets of the current framework. In case of a\nshock affecting vulnerable population, the authorities consider they can create space to provide\nsupport by reallocating spending away from public investment.\n\nExternal Sector Policies\n\n32. Policies for Higher and More Equitable Growth\n\n43.     Productivity growth has been weak despite successful trade opening and a stable\nmacroeconomic environment. The 1994 North American Free Trade Agreement (NAFTA)\neliminated almost all tariffs among Canada, Mexico, and the U.S. This opening reshaped the Mexican\neconomy, with exports as a share of GDP rising threefold. Foreign direct investments also increased.\nSimultaneously, cautious fiscal and monetary policies have achieved moderate inflation, exchange\nrate stability, and stable public debt. This growth strategy should have spurred investment,\nproductivity, and growth. Other supply side impediments, however, appear to have held back\nproductivity gains. 10\n\n                                                     Structural Indicators, 2021 or Latest Available Year\n The supply side policy agenda should be broadened to unlock Mexico’s growth\npotential. The current administration’s policy agenda has focused on redistribution, trade\npromotion, infrastructure investment, and trade integration to foster development, especially in\nSouthern states. Flagship reforms include (i) the implementation of the U.S.-Mexico-Canada\nAgreement (USMCA); (ii) increases in the minimum wage and the subcontracting law to reduce wage\ninequality; (iii) increases in the general social pension to reduce old age poverty; and (iv) the\ndevelopment of trade infrastructure in the Isthmus of Tehuantepec (“Isthmus corridor”) in the South\nof Mexico. While this agenda addresses some obstacles to higher productivity and growth,\nadditional efforts are needed. Priorities include corruption, crime and rule of law issues; addressing\nhuman capital and infrastructure shortfalls; and lessening labor and product market rigidities.\n\nA Budget for Higher, More Equitable Growth\n\n47.      A pivot to a more equitable and pro-growth budget would entail a gradual increase in\nproductive government spending, financed by revenue-increasing tax reforms. To lift the\ngrowth potential and reduce socioeconomic gaps, Mexico should address spending shortfalls in\neducation, health, public investment, and social safety nets (IMF Staff Report 2021/2020; IMF\nSelected Issues 2019, Paper 2; IMF WP/20/215). Structural indicators such as spending per student,\nthe teacher-to-student ratio, and public health spending per capita are well below the OECD\naverages and somewhat below emerging market averages (IMF WP/21/244). The pandemic has\nfurther increased spending needs, particularly in education (following 18 months of school closures).\nSeparately, there is scope, and a need, to increase revenue through tax reform, to support the\nadditional spending and secure tax buoyancy. 12\n\n48.      A gradual, permanent                                               Growth Decomposition\n                                                                         (Annual averages in percent)\nincrease in spending of around 2         4\n\nto 3 percent of GDP could foster         3\n\ninclusive growth, reduce                 2\npoverty, and address pandemic            1\nlegacies. For example, social assistance could reduce the leakage of benefits to high-\nincome groups and eliminate overlaps and coverage gaps with the creation of single beneficiary\n\n12 Based on staff’s macroeconomic forecasts, there is a fiscal gap of 0.2 percent of GDP in 2023, which gradually\n\nincreases to around 1.3 percent of GDP by 2027. In recent years, the authorities have closed expected gaps through\nrevenue and spending measures and small changes in the budget deficit target. The revenues generated from staff’s\nproposed tax reforms could also be used to close these gaps.\n\n\n\n24   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\nregistry. Efficiency gains in public investment could be achieved through better coordination across\ngovernment levels, effective national and sector strategies to guide planning, and better multi-year\nbudgeting (IMF 2019).\n\n49.     While higher oil prices have lowered immediate debt service pressures on Pemex,\nrestructuring efforts need to continue. Changes to the business plan should remain a priority,\nincluding focusing on production in profitable fields, selling non-core assets, and reforming its\npension scheme. More partnerships with private firms in upstream projects could help in\nmaintaining or increasing production capacity at lower cost and reducing risks to Pemex’s financial\nposition and the government budget.\n\n50.     A credible, well-designed medium-term tax reform could generate additional revenues\nto finance the permanent increase in spending on health, education and social assistance.\nMexico’s non-oil revenue was nearly 6 percent of GDP below Latin American peers (and only about\nhalf the OECD average) before the pandemic. Actions to strengthen tax administration should\ncontinue (Annex XII) but tax policy reforms would also be essential:\n\n•   Value Added Tax (VAT). Eliminating zero-ratings, except for a few essential foodstuffs, and\n    rationalizing exemptions and differences in rates increase collection. A comprehensive risk\n    management strategy and a high-coverage audit process for VAT returns could also reduce the\n    compliance gap.\n\n•   Personal income tax. Eliminating exclusions (e.g., of income on personal business activities and\n    independent services), reducing tax expenditure, and widening the top personal income tax\n    bracket. These measures could yield around ¾ percent of GDP.\n\n•   Subnational taxes. Property taxes could be increased gradually to raise over ¾ percent of GDP.\n    Updating the cadaster and enhancing policy coordination between the federal and subnational\n    governments would be essential. Simplifying and better enforcing the local vehicle tax could\n    increase revenue for states and municipalities.\n\n•   Carbon tax. While carbon pricing coverage through both the Emission Trading Scheme (ETS) and\n    carbon taxation is high relative to peers, Mexico could further raise the carbon price to levels\n    consistent with the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs; Black and others, 2021). The\n    carbon tax remains relatively narrow in scope with a de facto exemption of natural gas. 13 Also,\n    the tax rate (USD 3 per ton of CO2) is very low and should be raised to USD 75 by 2030 (along\n    the lines of the global carbon tax floor proposed for G20 economies by the IMF). Such a carbon\n    price, combined with an expansion of emissions pricing coverage, would raise additional\n    revenues of close to 1.8 percent of GDP by 2030 (Black and others, 2021). A compensation\n\n\n\n\n13 The carbon tax is an excise applied to the CO emissions of fossil fuel suppliers that exceed the emission rate of\n                                                2\nnatural gas.\n\n\n\n                                                                             INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND               25\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\n     scheme would be needed for those who are most negatively affected by the resulting energy\n     price increase.\n\n51.   Overall, the comprehensive fiscal package of higher spending financed with higher\nrevenues should have positive complementarities between growth and equity.\n\n•    The higher permanent spending aims to close above-discussed socioeconomic gaps that have\n     held back investment in human capital and productivity. In the context of Mexico where the\n     socioeconomic gaps are concentrated in specific regions, the increased and more efficient\n     productive spending, especially in those regions, should enhance both growth and equity.\n\n•    The trade-offs between growth and increased revenues are likely to be small as a significant part\n     come from VAT reforms, which should not distort factor supplies or affect long-term growth\n     especially since the reforms would be accompanied by compensation for poor households.\n     Increases in the personal income tax would largely be through simplifying and expanding the\n     tax base, which would have minimal effects on labor supply.\n\n•    Together, such budget reforms would increase growth and place public debt on a firm\n     downward trajectory over the medium term (IMF Staff Report 2021, IMF Staff Report 2020, IMF\n     WP/20/215).\n\nAuthorities’ Views\n\n52.     They are supportive of a pro-growth productive spending increase supported by tax\nreforms. But note such measures would require time to be implemented. In the meantime, they\nremain confident that the administrative measures to combat tax evasion and tax fraud taken by the\ncurrent administration would continue to maintain robust revenues.\n\nOther Structural Policies\n\n53.     A determined implementation of the anticorruption framework would help in\naddressing corruption and other governance issues. Contract enforcement by civil justice is very\nweak, discouraging business expansion and formal employment. This also complicates collateral\nseizure and impedes financial development. Corruption undermines the provision on public services.\nOrganized crime increases transport and insurance costs and discourages entrepreneurs and\nbusiness formation. Improving governance would unlock investment and growth potential. A new\nlaw treating corruption and fraud as felony offenses is expected to enable more comprehensive\ninvestigations on corruption. The challenge lies in implementing and assessing effectiveness of the\nanti-corruption policies, and in further coordinating state levels. Prevention, facilitation of reporting\nincluding with whistleblowing protection and further empowering the institutions in charge of\ninvestigation, prosecution and oversight can improve implementation. Mexico should participate in\nthe next round of voluntary assessments of transnational aspects of corruption by the Fund.\n\n\n\n\n26   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND", "excerpt_sha256": "312ed43dadb76a66e4f27fa4fc07f561ebca25c06afdb230c3779126ce1a524d", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt attributes the listed agenda to the current administration and identifies persistent priorities involving corruption, crime, rule of law, human capital, infrastructure, and labor and product-market rigidities."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0017-E006", "source_id": "SRC-065783efb0b09db2", "url": "https://www.banxico.org.mx/publications-and-press/imf-article-iv-consultation-for-mexico/%7B44F43B6B-61BD-D795-4561-1601FDD3FDB0%7D.pdf", "title": "Mexico: 2022 Article IV Consultation—Press Release and Staff Report", "publisher": "International Monetary Fund", "raw_sha256": "8fac4146590dbe2ed458f7356748f5880d021e03cc4982b1e7efe083d33f622a", "fact_summary": "The administration's energy policy replaced auctions with nonmarket-based policies and expanded the state electricity company's regulatory role, creating conflicts of interest, contract disruption, and policy uncertainty;
